On the Convergence of Min-Max Langevin Dynamics and Algorithm
Yang Cai, Siddharth Mitra, Xiuyuan Wang, Andre Wibisono

Abstract
We study zero-sum games in the space of probability distributions over the Euclidean space ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} with entropy regularization, in the setting when the interaction function between the players is smooth and strongly convex-strongly concave. We prove an exponential convergence guarantee for the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics to compute the equilibrium distribution of the zero-sum game. We also study the finite-particle approximation of the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics, both in continuous and discrete times. We prove biased convergence guarantees for the continuous-time finite-particle min-max Langevin dynamics to the stationary mean-field equilibrium distribution with an explicit bias term which does not scale with the number of particles. We also prove biased convergence guarantees for the discrete-time finite-particle min-max Langevin algorithm to the stationary mean-field equilibrium distribution with an additional bias term which scales with the step size and the number of particles. This provides an explicit iteration complexity for the average particle along the finite-particle algorithm to approximately compute the equilibrium distribution of the zero-sum game.



\coltauthor

Department of Computer Science, Yale University, New Haven, CT, USA

keywords
Zero-sum games, mean-field Langevin dynamics, finite-particle Langevin algorithm

1
Introduction

Many tasks in computer science, economics, and machine learning can be formulated as
games
in which two or more agents compete to optimize their own objective functions. Examples include Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs)
(
Goodfellow et al., 2020
)
, adversarial learning
(
Madry et al., 2018
)
, reinforcement learning
(
Busoniu et al., 2008
)
, and robust optimization
(
Rahimian and Mehrotra, 2022
)
. The solutions to these problems correspond to the Nash equilibria of these games.

Two-player zero-sum games, first studied by
Borel (1921)
and
v. Neumann (1928)
, are arguably the most fundamental and well-studied class of games in game theory. Unlike classical game theory, where players are typically assumed to choose actions from a finite set, modern applications – including those mentioned above – require players to select actions from a continuous set
\mathcal{X}
, introducing substantial new challenges in both the existence and tractability of Nash equilibria.

Given a zero-sum game over action sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
with payoff function
V\colon\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}V(x,y),

(1)

the celebrated Minimax Theorem by
v. Neumann (1928)
guarantees the existence of a pure (Nash) equilibrium
(x^{*},y^{*})
of this game if
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are compact convex sets, and
V(x,y)
is convex in
x
and concave in
y
.
1
1

1

The finite-action setting is captured by choosing
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
as simplices and
V(x,y)
as a bilinear function.
Unfortunately, when the payoff function is general, such a pure equilibrium need not exist. This motivates us to consider the more general solution concept of a mixed (Nash) equilibrium, which is a probability distribution over actions, and which is guaranteed to exist even for general payoff functions
(
Glicksberg, 1952
)
.
The problem of finding mixed equilibria in game (
1
) can be recast as finding pure equilibria in the lifted game over the spaces of probability distributions
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
, where the payoff function is given by the expectation of the base payoff function:

\displaystyle\min_{\rho^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\max_{\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\rho^{Y}}[V(X,Y)].

(2)

This has been studied in many works, including in
(
Hsieh et al., 2019
)
for GANs.
The actions in the lifted game (
2
) are probability distributions
\rho^{X}
and
\rho^{Y}
over the base sets
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, so a pure equilibrium for the lifted game (
2
) corresponds to a mixed equilibrium of the base game (
1
);
we refer to a pure equilibrium of the lifted game (
2
) as an
equilibrium distribution
.

Despite its universality, finding an equilibrium distribution for the game (
2
) may still be computationally challenging, depending on the base function
V
.
Recent works, including
(
Hsieh et al., 2019
;
Domingo-Enrich et al., 2020
;
Ma and Ying, 2022
;
Lu, 2023
;
Ding et al., 2024
;
Kim et al., 2024
)
, have proposed regularizing the game by adding an entropy term to the payoff function with some regularization parameter
\tau>0
;
this results in a game (
3
) that we study below.
The hope is that the entropy regularization makes the equilibrium distribution easier to compute, similar to what happens in the finite-dimensional problem.

In the game theory literature, the equilibrium distribution of the regularized game (
3
) with entropy regularization is known as the
quantal response equilibrium
(QRE) defined by
McKelvey and Palfrey (1995)
.
2
2

2

QRE is originally defined for finite action games. We study its natural extension to continuous games in this paper.

When the regularization parameter
\tau>0
is small, the QRE provides a good approximation to the equilibrium distribution of the original game (
2
).
We also observe that the QRE is the solution to one step of the proximal method with entropy regularization to compute the equilibrium distribution of the original game (
2
); therefore, if we can compute the QRE efficiently, then we can hope to run the conceptual prox method
(
Nemirovski, 2004
)
which may have a good convergence property to the true equilibrium distribution of the game (
2
).

In this paper, we study this question in the setting when the payoff function
V
is strongly convex-strongly concave.
Two remarks are in order. First, in this setting, a pure equilibrium, i.e., an equilibrium distribution that is a point mass, of the game (
2
) exists and is tractable
(
Facchinei and Pang, 2003
)
.
However, these algorithms apply only to finite-dimensional settings, making them unsuitable for finding the QRE, an infinite-dimensional object.
Second, in the space of probability distributions under the Wasserstein metric, the payoff function of the regularized game (
3
) is geodesically strongly convex-strongly concave.
In finite dimensions, for a strongly convex-strongly concave min-max optimization problem, the min-max gradient flow and its straightforward discretization – the gradient descent-ascent algorithm – converge at an exponential rate
(
Facchinei and Pang, 2003
, Chapter 12.4.2)
; see also Section
H
for a review of the deterministic game.
In our setting, the min-max Wasserstein gradient flow in the space of distributions corresponds to the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics, arguably the most natural dynamics for solving the game.
Unlike in the finite-dimensional case, the convergence properties of this natural dynamics, along with its particle and time discretizations, remain unknown and were listed as an open question in
(
Wang and Chizat, 2024
)
.
All existing results for finding the QRE are for different settings or require modifications to the algorithms – see review below.
This gap in our understanding motivates us to investigate the following question:

Does the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics
converge for the regularized game (
3
)? If so, what are the convergence rates of its particle approximation and its discrete-time implementation?

Related work

Zero-sum games in the space of probability distributions have been studied in many recent works, including
(
Hsieh et al., 2019
;
Domingo-Enrich et al., 2020
;
Cen et al., 2021
;
Wang and Chizat, 2022
;
Ma and Ying, 2022
;
Lu, 2023
;
Kim et al., 2024
;
Lascu et al., 2023
;
Lascu et al., 2024a
;
Lascu et al., 2024b
;
Ding et al., 2024
;
Conger et al., 2024
;
An and Lu, 2025
;
Lu and Monmarché, 2025
)
.
We mention a few works here, and refer the reader to Section
A
for further discussion.
When the domains are compact Riemannian manifolds,

Domingo-Enrich et al. (2020)
show that if the mean-field dynamics converges, then it must converge to the equilibrium distribution.

Ma and Ying (2022)
and
Lu (2023)
show the convergence of the continuous-time mean-field dynamics under timescale separation.

Conger et al. (2024)
study the more general setting of min-max and cooperative games in the space of distributions.
Notably,
(
Conger et al., 2024
, Theorem 3.4)
show that for zero-sum games under strong convexity, the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics has exponential convergence to the equilibrium distribution; thus, they have answered the open question by
(
Wang and Chizat, 2024
)
in the continuous-time mean-field setting under strong convexity.
In this work, we complement the results by providing guarantees for the finite-particle dynamics and the discrete-time algorithm.
When the domains are Euclidean spaces,

Kim et al. (2024)
study zero-sum games with entropy regularization with a more general convex-concave interaction functional using the mean-field Langevin dynamics with a modified drift term replaced by the time average of the gradients; they prove a continuous-time convergence rate, as well as a convergence analysis of the finite-particle discrete-time algorithm.

Ding et al. (2024)
study a finite-particle discrete-time algorithm that implements the mirror-prox primal-dual algorithm in the space of distributions,
and show explicit convergence guarantees of the resulting algorithm, under the assumption that the base payoff function is a bounded perturbation of a quadratic function.

1.1
Problem Setting

In this paper, we study zero-sum games in the space of probability distributions with interaction term which is an expectation over a base interaction function, with entropy regularization.
We work on the unconstrained state space
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for some dimension
d\geq 1
.
Our techniques and results also generalize to
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{Y}}
where
d_{X}\neq d_{Y}
, but here we set
d_{X}=d_{Y}=d
for simplicity.
Let
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of probability distributions over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and have a finite second moment.
Let
H(\rho)=-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\log\rho]
be the entropy functional.

Let
V\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a given base payoff function.
We study the following min-max (or zero-sum) game on the product space of probability distributions:

\displaystyle\min_{\rho^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\max_{\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\;\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})

(3)

where
\tau>0
is a regularization parameter, and the function
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}):=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\rho^{Y}}[V]-\tau H(\rho^{X})+\tau H(\rho^{Y}).

(4)

We say that a pair of probability distributions
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an
equilibrium distribution
for the min-max game (
3
) if the following holds for all
(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\bar{\nu}^{X},\rho^{Y})\leq\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})\leq\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y}).

(5)

The
duality gap

\mathsf{DG}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
of the min-max game (
3
) is defined by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DG}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}):=\max_{\tilde{\rho}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\tilde{\rho}^{Y})-\min_{\tilde{\rho}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}^{X},\rho^{Y}).

(6)

In the literature, this is also known as the Nikaido-Isoda error
(
Nikaidô and Isoda, 1955
)
.
Note that by construction,
\mathsf{DG}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})\geq 0
for all
\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and furthermore,
\mathsf{DG}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})=0
if and only if
(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})
is an equilibrium distribution for the game (
3
).

We are interested in characterizing the existence and uniqueness of the equilibrium distribution, as well as algorithms for approximately computing the equilibrium distribution in practice.
We study the case when the payoff function
V(x,y)
is strongly convex in
x
, strongly concave in
y
, and has bounded second derivatives, see Assumption
1
.
In this case, there exists a unique equilibrium distribution
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})
; see Section
3
.

We now provide details for the dynamics and algorithms we study.

(1) The mean-field dynamics:

This is a pair of stochastic processes
(\bar{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\bar{Y}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which evolve via the following
mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics
for all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle d\bar{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})]\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{X}

(7a)

\displaystyle d\bar{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})]\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{Y}

(7b)

where
\bar{X}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
, and
where
(W_{t}^{X})_{t\geq 0}
and
(W_{t}^{Y})_{t\geq 0}
are independent standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This comes from running the gradient flow dynamics in the space of probability distributions with the Wasserstein metric; see Section
C.1
for derivation.
The dynamics (
7
) is a
mean-field
system because the evolution of
\bar{X}_{t}
depends on the distribution
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
, and similarly, the evolution of
\bar{Y}_{t}
depends on
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}
.
However, note the dependence is only via their expectation, so in the mean-field system above,
\bar{X}_{t}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}
evolve independently.
Therefore, if we initialize from independent
(\bar{X}_{0},\bar{Y}_{0})\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}
, then
(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
remains independent for all
t\geq 0
.

Main Result 1:

When the payoff function
V
is strongly concave-strongly convex, we show the mean-field dynamics
(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X},\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y})
converges to the equilibrium distribution
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})
exponentially fast in terms of duality gap, KL divergence, and Wasserstein distance; see Theorem
3
.

However, the mean-field system is an idealized algorithm that we cannot exactly implement due to its mean-field dependence and continuous-time nature. Thus, we study its approximations.

(2) The particle dynamics:

This is an approximation of the mean-field dynamics (
7
) by replacing
\bar{X}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
by
N\geq 1
particles
\mathbf{X}_{t}=(X_{t}^{1},\dots,X_{t}^{N})\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{X}}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(Y_{t}^{1},\dots,Y_{t}^{N})\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Y}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{dN}
, which jointly evolve via the following, for all
t\geq 0
and for all
i\in[N]:=\{1,\dots,N\}
:

\displaystyle dX_{t}^{i}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(X_{t}^{i},Y_{t}^{j})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{X,i}

(8a)

\displaystyle dY_{t}^{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{y}V(X_{t}^{j},Y_{t}^{i})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{Y,i}

(8b)

where
(W_{t}^{X,i})_{t\geq 0}
and
(W_{t}^{Y,i})_{t\geq 0}
are independent standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for
i\in[N]
.
Note the above is not a mean-field system, but a standard system of interacting stochastic processes.
The distribution of
\mathbf{Z}_{t}:=(\mathbf{X}_{t},\mathbf{Y}_{t})\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
is in general not independent:
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\neq\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Y}}
.
Note that in the particle dynamics (
8
), we use the empirical mean from the
N
particles to approximate the true expectation in the mean-field dynamics.
Thus, as
N\to\infty
, we expect the particle dynamics (
8
) to become closer to the mean-field dynamics.
As
t\to\infty
, the particle dynamics (
8
) converges to a stationary distribution
(\mathbf{X}_{\infty},\mathbf{Y}_{\infty})\sim\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, which we expect to be close to the independent product of the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{X})^{\otimes N}\otimes(\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{\otimes N}
.

Main Result 2:

For strongly convex-strongly concave and smooth
V
,
we prove a biased convergence guarantee of
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
along the finite-particle dynamics (
8
) to the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, with a bias that is independent of the number of particles
N
; see Theorem
4
.

(3) The particle algorithm:

This is a time discretization of the particle dynamics (
8
), which maintains a collection of particles

\mathbf{x}_{k}=(x_{k}^{1},\dots,x_{k}^{N})\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{x},\eta}
and
\mathbf{y}_{k}=(y_{k}^{1},\dots,y_{k}^{N})\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{y},\eta}
in
\mathbb{R}^{dN}
and evolves them via the following discrete-time update, for all
k\geq 0
and for all
i\in[N]
:

\displaystyle x_{k+1}^{i}

\displaystyle=x_{k}^{i}-\eta\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(x_{k}^{i},y_{k}^{j})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\zeta_{k}^{x,i}

(9a)

\displaystyle y_{k+1}^{i}

\displaystyle=y_{k}^{i}+\eta\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{y}V(x_{k}^{j},y_{k}^{i})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\zeta_{k}^{y,i}

(9b)

where
\eta>0
is a fixed step size, and
\zeta_{k}^{x,1},\dots,\zeta_{k}^{x,N},\zeta_{k}^{y,1},\dots,\zeta_{k}^{x,N}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
are independent standard Gaussian random variables in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Similar to standard discretization of the Langevin dynamics, this discrete-time algorithm is biased, i.e., as
k\to\infty
, the distribution of
\mathbf{z}_{k}=(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k})\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
converges to some distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
which is different from the stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
of the continuous-time particle dynamics (
8
).

Main Result 3:

For strongly convex-strongly concave and smooth
V
, we prove a biased convergence guarantee for
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
along the discrete-time algorithm (
9
) to the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, with
a bias that scales with step size
\eta
and the number of particles
N
; see Theorem
6
.
This provides an explicit iteration complexity guarantee for the average particle of the
algorithm (
9
) to approximate
the equilibrium distribution
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
for the game (
3
); see Corollary
8
.

Organization:

We provide definitions and assumptions in Section
2
.
We discuss the properties of the equilibrium distribution and the duality gap in Section
3
.
We discuss the convergence analysis of the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics in Section
4
.
We discuss the convergence analysis of the continuous-time finite-particle min-max Langevin dynamics in Section
5
, and the discrete-time finite-particle min-max Langevin algorithm in Section
6
.
We conclude with discussion in Section
7
.
We provide additional details and proofs in the appendix.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation and Definitions

Let
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of probability distributions
\rho
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and which have finite second moment:
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\|X\|^{2}]<\infty
.
We identify a probability distribution
\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with its probability density function (Radon-Nikodym derivative)
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,\infty)
with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

Let
H\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
be the
entropy
functional:

H(\rho)=-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\log\rho]=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(x)\,\log\rho(x)\,dx.

The Wasserstein
W_{2}
distance between probability distributions
\rho,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by:

W_{2}(\rho,\nu)=\inf_{\gamma\in\Pi(\rho,\nu)}\mathbb{E}\left[\|X-Y\|^{2}\right]^{1/2},

where the infimum is over all couplings between
\rho
and
\nu
, i.e., joint distributions of
(X,Y)\sim\gamma
with the correct marginal distributions
X\sim\rho
and
Y\sim\nu
.

For probability distributions
\rho,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\rho\ll\nu
(i.e., if
\nu(x)=0
, then
\rho(x)=0
), the
Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence
or the
relative entropy
of
\rho
with respect to
\nu
is defined by:

\mathsf{KL}(\rho\,\|\,\nu)=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\log\frac{\rho}{\nu}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(x)\log\frac{\rho(x)}{\nu(x)}\,dx.

If
\rho,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
have differentiable density functions, then the
relative Fisher information
of
\rho
with respect to
\nu
is defined by:

\mathsf{FI}(\rho\,\|\,\nu)=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\rho}{\nu}\right\|^{2}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho(x)\,\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\rho(x)}{\nu(x)}\right\|^{2}\,dx.

We say a probability distribution
\nu
is
\alpha
-strongly log-concave
(
\alpha
-SLC) for some
\alpha>0
if the negative log-density
-\log\nu\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\alpha
-strongly convex; if
\log\nu
is twice differentiable, then this is equivalent to
-\nabla^{2}\log\nu(x)\succeq\alpha I
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We say
\nu
satisfies
\alpha
-log-Sobolev inequality
(
\alpha
-LSI) for some
\alpha>0
if for all probability distribution
\rho
, the following inequality holds:

\mathsf{FI}(\rho\,\|\,\nu)\geq 2\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho\,\|\,\nu).

We say
\nu
satisfies
\alpha
-Talagrand inequality
(
\alpha
-TI) for some
\alpha>0
if for all probability distribution
\rho
, the following inequality holds:

\mathsf{KL}(\rho\,\|\,\nu)\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}\,W_{2}(\rho,\nu)^{2}.

We recall that if
\nu
is
\alpha
-SLC, then
\nu
also satisfies
\alpha
-LSI;
furthermore, if
\nu
satisfies
\alpha
-LSI, then
\nu
also satisfies
\alpha
-TI, see
(
Otto and Villani, 2000
)
.

2.2
Assumptions

Throughout this paper, we make the following assumption.

Assumption 1
.

The base interaction function
V\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is four-times continuously differentiable,
\alpha
-strongly convex in the first argument,
\alpha
-strongly concave in the second argument,
and is
L
-smooth of the second order, for some
0<\alpha\leq L<\infty
.
That is, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{xx}V(x,y)

\displaystyle\succeq\alpha I,\qquad-\nabla^{2}_{yy}V(x,y)\succeq\alpha I,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\|\nabla^{2}V(x,y)\|_{\mathsf{op}}\leq L.

In particular,
(x,y)\mapsto\nabla V(x,y)
is
L
-Lipschitz for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We note the assumption that
V
be four-times differentiable is to ensure that our computations below are justified, in particular when using integration by parts such as in the proof of Lemma
9
.

3
Properties of the Equilibrium Distribution and Duality Gap

We define the
best-response maps

\Phi^{X}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
and
\Phi^{Y}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
by:

\displaystyle\Phi^{X}(\rho^{Y})=\nu^{X}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Phi^{Y}(\rho^{X})=\nu^{Y}

for all
\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\nu^{X},\nu^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are distributions with density given by:

\displaystyle\nu^{X}(x)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{Y}}[V(x,Y)]\right),

(10a)

\displaystyle\nu^{Y}(y)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}[V(X,y)]\right).

(10b)

Note that if we assume
V
is strongly convex-strongly concave, then the maps above are well-defined, since the right-hand sides are integrable over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
More generally, we need some growth condition on
V
to ensure the above are well-defined.
The term best-response is justified by the following property.
We provide the proof of Lemma
1
in Section
B.1
.

Lemma 1
.

For all
\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\nu^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\rho^{Y})
and
\nu^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\rho^{X})
, we have:

\displaystyle\nu^{X}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{\tilde{\rho}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}^{X},\rho^{Y}),

\displaystyle\nu^{Y}

\displaystyle=\arg\max_{\tilde{\rho}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\tilde{\rho}^{Y}).

Furthermore, the duality gap is given by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DG}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})=\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\nu^{X})+\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\nu^{Y}).

Therefore, the equilibrium distribution
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})
, which minimizes the duality gap, is a fixed point of the best-response maps:

\displaystyle\Phi^{X}(\bar{\nu}^{Y})=\bar{\nu}^{X}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Phi^{Y}(\bar{\nu}^{X})=\bar{\nu}^{Y}.

We have the following property, which guarantees that if we can minimize the duality gap, then we also have convergence of the iterates to the equilibrium distribution, both in KL divergence and in Wasserstein distance.
The uniqueness of the equilibrium distribution follows from our convergence guarantee of the mean-field dynamics presented in Theorem
3
.
The bound (
11
) below is the same as stated in
(
Wang and Chizat, 2024
, Eq. (4))
and
(
Kim et al., 2024
, Lemma 3.5)
;
the first inequality in (
11
) follows from Talagrand’s inequality, and the second inequality follows from the optimality property of the duality gap.
We provide the proof of Lemma
2
in Section
B.2
.

Lemma 2
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then there exists a unique equilibrium distribution
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the game (
3
), and the distributions
\bar{\nu}^{X}
and
\bar{\nu}^{Y}
are
(\alpha/\tau)
-strongly log-concave.
Furthermore, for all
(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(W_{2}(\rho^{X},\bar{\nu}^{X})^{2}+W_{2}(\rho^{Y},\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{2}\right)\leq\tau\left(\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})+\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y})\right)\leq\mathsf{DG}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}).

(11)

4
Analysis of the Mean-Field Min-Max Langevin Dynamics

Given a pair of random variables
\bar{X}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can define the joint random variable
\bar{Z}_{t}=(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
which has an independent joint distribution
\bar{Z}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}:=\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
.
Define the independent product of the best-response distributions

\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}:=\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}

where
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y})
and
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X})
.
We observe that if
\bar{X}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
evolve following the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics (
7
),
then the joint variable
\bar{Z}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
evolves following the Langevin dynamics targeting its best-response distribution
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}
:

\displaystyle d\bar{Z}_{t}=\tau\nabla\log\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}(\bar{Z}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{Z}

(12)

where
W_{t}^{Z}=(W_{t}^{X},W_{t}^{Y})
is the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
This follows from the definition of the best-response distributions (
10
) and computing their score functions.

We have the following convergence results for the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics.
We show that the relative Fisher information between the iterates and their best-response distributions converges exponentially fast along the mean-field Langevin dynamics (Theorem
3
part (1)).
This is analogous to the result in the deterministic setting where the squared norm of the velocity vector field converges exponentially fast along the min-max gradient flow (see Theorem
35
in Section
H
for a review).
For the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics, which is the min-max Wasserstein gradient flow, the squared Wasserstein norm of the velocity of
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
is precisely the relative Fisher information between
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
and its best-response distribution
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}
, which motivates our result below.
The convergence in relative Fisher information also implies the convergence in the duality gap or KL divergence, by the log-Sobolev inequality (Theorem
3
part (2)).

Below, we write
\mathsf{DG}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z})\equiv\mathsf{DG}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X},\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y})
.
We provide the proof of Theorem
3
in Section
C.4
.

Theorem 3
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\bar{Z}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
evolves following the mean-field dynamics (
12
) in the joint space
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
from
\bar{Z}_{0}\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, and let
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}
be the best-response distribution as defined above.
Then for all
t\geq 0
, we have the following properties:

1.

Convergence in relative Fisher information:

\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}).

2.

Convergence in duality gap:

\mathsf{DG}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z})=\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\frac{\tau^{2}}{2\alpha}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}).

Let
\bar{\nu}^{Z}:=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}
be the stationary distribution for the mean-field dynamics in the joint space
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Note that by the bound (
11
) from Lemma
2
, the convergence rate in duality gap above also implies the following convergence guarantees between the iterate
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
of the mean-field dynamics and the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2}\,W_{2}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z},\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}\,\leq\,\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\,\leq\,\mathsf{DG}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z})\,\leq\,e^{-2\alpha t}\,\frac{\tau^{2}}{2\alpha}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}).

(13)

The bound above is in terms of the initial relative Fisher information of
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}
to its best-response distribution.
When we choose a Gaussian initial distribution
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\tau^{2}}{L^{2}}I)
, we can bound
\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})=O(\frac{dL^{2}}{\tau^{2}})
; see Lemma
11
in Section
C.4.1
.

We note that under Assumption
1
, one can show that the mean-field dynamics (
12
) is in fact an exponential contraction in the
W_{2}
distance, which implies
W_{2}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z},\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z},\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}
, see
(
Conger et al., 2024
, Proposition 7.3)
.
We note that
(
Conger et al., 2024
, Lemma 7.2)
also shows the exponential convergence of the dissipation functional, which is the same as the relative Fisher information above in our setting.
We provide a self-contained proof of Theorem
3
in Section
C.4
; in particular, we derive an identity of the time derivative of the relative Fisher information along the mean-field Langevin dynamics, see Lemma
9
in Section
C.2
.

5
Analysis of the Finite-Particle Min-Max Langevin Dynamics

Here we study a finite-particle approximation of the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics (
7
) where we replace each
\bar{X}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
N\geq 1
particles
X_{t}^{1},\dots,X_{t}^{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
Y_{t}^{1},\dots,Y_{t}^{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which evolve following the finite-particle system of dynamics (
8
) where we use the empirical mean from the particles in place of the true expectation in the drift terms of (
7
).

We can write the finite-particle dynamics (
8
) in terms of the joint vectors
\mathbf{X}_{t}=(X_{t}^{1},\dots,X_{t}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(Y_{t}^{1},\dots,Y_{t}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
as:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X}_{t},\mathbf{Y}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{X}}

(14a)

\displaystyle d\mathbf{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{X}_{t},\mathbf{Y}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Y}}

(14b)

where
(W_{t}^{\mathbf{X}})_{t\geq 0}
and
(W_{t}^{\mathbf{Y}})_{t\geq 0}
are independent standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}^{dN}
.
In the above, we have defined the vector fields
b^{\mathbf{X}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{dN}\times\mathbb{R}^{dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{dN}
and
b^{\mathbf{Y}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{dN}\times\mathbb{R}^{dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{dN}
by, for all
\mathbf{x}=(x^{1},\dots,x^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
and
\mathbf{y}=(y^{1},\dots,y^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
:

\displaystyle b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}b^{X}(x^{1},\mathbf{y})\\
\cdots\\
b^{X}(x^{N},\mathbf{y})\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(x^{1},y^{j})\\
\cdots\\
-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(x^{N},y^{j})\end{pmatrix},

(15a)

\displaystyle b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}b^{Y}(\mathbf{x},y^{1})\\
\cdots\\
b^{Y}(\mathbf{x},y^{N})\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{y}V(x^{j},y^{1})\\
\cdots\\
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{y}V(x^{j},y^{N})\end{pmatrix}.

(15b)

We can further write the finite-particle dynamics (
14
) in terms of the joint random variable
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=(\mathbf{X}_{t},\mathbf{Y}_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
as:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{Z}_{t}=b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

(16)

where
W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(W_{t}^{\mathbf{X}},W_{t}^{\mathbf{Y}})
is the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, and we have defined the vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
by, for all
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\displaystyle b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})=\begin{pmatrix}b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\\
b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\end{pmatrix}.

(17)

5.1
Biased Convergence of Finite-Particle Dynamics to Stationary Mean-Field Distribution

Recall
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is the stationary distribution for the base mean-field dynamics (
7
).
We define the tensorized stationary mean-field distribution:

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}:=(\bar{\nu}^{X})^{\otimes N}\otimes(\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{\otimes N}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN}).

(18)

We can show the biased convergence guarantee of the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) to the tensorized mean-field stationary distribution (
18
) in Theorem
4
.
A key observation is that the drift term in the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) is dissipative (see Lemma
12
in Section
D.1
); this allows us to perform a synchronous coupling analysis against the stationary mean-field dynamics to show a biased convergence guarantee in
W_{2}
distance, which we leverage to show a biased convergence guarantee in KL divergence via a time derivative calculation along simultaneous diffusion processes.
A careful calculation shows that we can control the bias without dependence on the number of particles
N
(see Lemma
15
in Section
D.2
).
We provide the proof of Theorem
4
in Section
E.1
.

Theorem 4
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
from
\bar{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
Then for all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha t}\left(\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

By taking
t\to\infty
, we obtain the following estimates on the bias of the stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
of the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) from the tensorized stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

We can bound the variance by
\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\leq(2\tau d)/\alpha
, see Lemma
24
. Therefore, note that the bias terms above do not scale with the number of particles
N
.
When we consider the average particle below, this implies a bias of order
O(1/N)
, see Corollary
5
.

We can also show the unbiased exponential convergence guarantees of the iterate
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
of the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) to its stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, see Theorem
33
in Section
G.2
.

5.2
Biased Convergence of the Average Particle of the Finite-Particle Dynamics

Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=(\mathbf{X}_{t},\mathbf{Y}_{t})=(X_{t}^{1},\dots,X_{t}^{N},Y_{t}^{1},\dots,Y_{t}^{N})\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
We define the
average particle
to be the random variable
Z_{t}^{I}=(X_{t}^{I},Y_{t}^{I})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, where
I\sim\mathrm{Unif}([N])
is a uniformly chosen random index.
Note the distribution of the average particle
Z_{t}^{I}\sim\rho_{t}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}
(including the randomization over indices) is:

\rho_{t}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\rho_{t}^{Z,i}

where
\rho_{t}^{Z,i}
is the marginal distribution of
Z_{t}^{i}=(X_{t}^{i},Y_{t}^{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
from the joint vector
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.

We have the following biased convergence guarantee of the average particle along the finite-particle dynamics (
16
).
We provide the proof of Corollary
5
in Section
E.2
.

Corollary 5
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, and let
Z_{t}^{I}\sim\rho_{t}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}
be the average particle as defined above.
For all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\leq\frac{1}{N}e^{-\alpha t}\left(\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau N}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

By taking
t\to\infty
, we see that the limiting distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}
of the average particle satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{\infty}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\leq\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau N}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

This shows that as
N\to\infty
and
t\to\infty
, the average particle of the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) indeed converges to the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
, which is the equilibrium distribution for the game (
3
) that we wish to compute.
However, this is still in the idealized continuous-time setting.
To obtain a meaningful practical guarantee, we study the discrete-time algorithm in Section
6
.

6
Analysis of the Discrete-Time Finite-Particle Min-Max Langevin Algorithm

We study a time discretization of the finite-particle dynamics (
8
) into the discrete-time algorithm (
9
).
We can write the algorithm (
9
) in terms of the joint vectors
\mathbf{x}_{k}=(x_{k}^{1},\dots,x_{k}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
and
\mathbf{y}_{k}=(y_{k}^{1},\dots,y_{k}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
which follow the update:

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}_{k}+\eta b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{x}}

(19a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}_{k}+\eta b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{y}}

(19b)

where
\eta>0
is step size,
\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{x}},\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{y}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
are independent standard Gaussian random variables in
\mathbb{R}^{dN}
, and where we have used the same vector fields
b^{\mathbf{X}}
and
b^{\mathbf{Y}}
as defined in (
15
).

We can further write the algorithm (
19
) in terms of the joint random variable
\mathbf{z}_{k}=(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
which follows the update:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}_{k}+\eta b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{k})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{z}}

(20)

where
\eta>0
is step size,
\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is an independent standard Gaussian random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, and where we have used the same vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
as defined in (
17
).

6.1
Biased Convergence of Finite-Particle Algorithm to Stationary Mean-Field Distribution

We can prove the following biased convergence guarantees of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) to the tensorized stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
defined in (
18
).
Our technique is to show a one-step biased contraction guarantee in
W_{2}
distance along the discrete-time algorithm (
20
), using a continuous-time interpolation of each step of the algorithm (
20
) as the solution to a stochastic process, and performing a synchronous coupling analysis against the stationary mean-field dynamics; see Lemma
22
in Section
F.2.1
.
We leverage this to show a one-step biased contraction guarantee in KL divergence along the algorithm (
20
), see Lemma
23
in Section
F.2.2
, and solve the recursions to conclude the biased convergence guarantees stated in Theorem
6
.
We provide the proof of Theorem
6
in Section
F.3
.

Note that the bias terms in Theorem
6
below match the continuous-time bias from Theorem
4
, with an additional bias term that scales with the step size
\eta
as well as the number of particles
N
, which comes from the dimension of the space
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
where the algorithm operates.
This additional bias term that scales with
\eta
is consistent with the analysis of simple discretization of the Langevin dynamics such as the Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm, see e.g.,
(
Vempala and Wibisono, 2019
)
.

Theorem 6
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
evolves via the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
with step size
0<\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
from
\mathbf{z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
Then for all
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta k}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{8L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right)

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}\left(\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+\frac{45L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+55\,\eta\tau dN\right).

As the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) is practically implementable, this provides a concrete algorithm to approximately compute the stationary mean-field distribution, with an explicit iteration complexity which we characterize in Corollary
8
below.

We can also show the unbiased exponential convergence guarantees of the iterate
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) to its stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
, see Theorem
34
in Section
G.3
.

6.2
Biased Convergence of the Average Particle of the Finite-Particle Algorithm

Suppose
\mathbf{z}_{k}=(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k})=(x_{k}^{1},\dots,x_{k}^{N},y_{k}^{1},\dots,y_{k}^{N})\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
evolves following the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
As before, we define the
average particle
to be the random variable
z_{k}^{I}=(x_{k}^{I},y_{k}^{I})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, where
I\sim\mathrm{Unif}([N])
is a uniformly chosen random index.
The distribution of the average particle
z_{k}^{I}\sim\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}
(including the randomization over indices) is:

\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}

where
\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}
is the marginal distribution of
z_{k}^{i}=(x_{k}^{i},y_{k}^{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
from the joint vector
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.

We have the following biased convergence guarantee of the average particle along the finite-particle algorithm (
20
).
We provide the proof of Corollary
7
in Section
F.4
.

Corollary 7
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
evolves following the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
with step size
0<\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
, and let
z_{k}^{I}\sim\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}
be the average particle as defined above.
Then for all
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\leq\frac{e^{-\alpha\eta k}}{N}\left(\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+\frac{45L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau N}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+2475\frac{\eta dL^{4}}{\alpha^{3}}.

By taking
k\to\infty
, we see that the limiting distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}
of the average particle satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{\infty}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\leq 45\frac{L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau N}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+2475\frac{\eta dL^{4}}{\alpha^{3}}.

This shows that the average particle of the
N
-particle algorithm approximately converges to the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
, up to a bias term which scales with step size
\eta
and inversely with the number of particles
1/N
.
Thus, we can make the bias arbitrarily small by choosing a sufficiently large number of particles
N
and a sufficiently small step size
\eta
.

The result above implies the following iteration complexity guarantee for the average particle of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) to reach an arbitrary precision in KL divergence to the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}
, which is the equilibrium distribution for the game (
3
) that we wish to compute.
We provide the proof of Corollary
8
in Section
F.5.3
.

Corollary 8
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
For any regularization parameter
\tau>0
, and given any small error threshold
\varepsilon>0
, suppose we do the following:

1.

Run the min-max gradient descent algorithm (
65
) from
\tilde{z}_{0}=(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with step size
\eta_{\mathsf{GD}}=\frac{\alpha}{4L^{2}}
for

k_{\mathsf{GD}}\geq\frac{4L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{\alpha^{3}\|z^{*}\|^{2}}{\tau dL^{2}}

iterations, to obtain a final point
m^{Z}=\tilde{z}_{k_{\mathsf{GD}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.

2.

Define the Gaussian distribution
\gamma^{Z}=\mathcal{N}(m^{Z},\frac{\tau}{L}I)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, and initialize the algorithm (
20
) from
\mathbf{z}_{0}=(z_{0}^{1},\dots,z_{0}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}

where
z_{0}^{1},\dots,z_{0}^{N}\sim\gamma^{Z}
are i.i.d., so
\mathbf{z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=(\gamma^{Z})^{\otimes N}
.

3.

Run the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size and number of particles:

\eta=\frac{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{3}}{7500\,dL^{4}}\ ,\qquad\qquad N\geq\frac{270\,dL^{4}}{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{4}}.

Then the average particle
z_{k}^{I}\sim\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}
of the algorithm (
20
) satisfies
\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\leq\varepsilon

whenever the number of iterations
k
satisfies:

k\,\geq\,\frac{7500\,dL^{4}}{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{684\,dL^{6}}{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{6}}.

7
Discussion

In this paper, we study zero-sum games in the space of probability distributions over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with entropy regularization and a base interaction function which is smooth and strongly convex-strongly concave.
We show the exponential convergence guarantee of the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics to the equilibrium distribution in continuous time.
We also show the biased convergence guarantees for the finite-particle dynamics in continuous time, and the finite-particle algorithm in discrete time, to the equilibrium (stationary mean-field) distribution.
We also provide an explicit iteration complexity for the average particle of the finite-particle algorithm to approximately compute the equilibrium distribution.
We use standard tools from the analysis of stochastic processes and their time discretization, which have been used for analyzing sampling algorithms.

Our results answer a special case of the open question posed by
(
Wang and Chizat, 2024
)
in the simple setting of Euclidean case under strong convexity assumption.
There are many directions one can study
toward the more general open question.
It would be interesting to study how to extend our results to weaken the strong convexity assumption, for example to allow weak convexity or local non-convexity of the interaction function, or only assuming isoperimetry of the equilibrium distribution.
In our analysis, the strong convexity assumption is crucial to show the exponential convergence guarantee of the mean-field dynamics and to provide biased convergence guarantees for the finite-particle systems
in
W_{2}
distance, which we leverage to obtain biased convergence guarantees in KL divergence.
It would also be interesting to study the more general setting of constrained domains or the manifold setting without convexity assumptions.
For constrained domains, we may have to add a reflection or projection step to the Langevin dynamics; or we can try to adapt the idea of the Proximal Sampler from sampling, see e.g.,
(
Lee et al., 2021
;
Chen et al., 2022
)
.

acknowledgments-disclosure-of-funding.
The authors thank Guillaume Wang, Daniel Lacker, and Manuel Arnese for valuable discussions.
A.W. thanks Tinsel W. for guidance. A.W. is supported by NSF Award CCF-2403391 and CCF-2443097.
Y.C. is supported by the NSF Award CCF-2342642.

Appendix A
Additional Related Work

Zero-sum games in the space of probability distributions have been studied in many recent works, including
(
Hsieh et al., 2019
;
Domingo-Enrich et al., 2020
;
Cen et al., 2021
;
Wang and Chizat, 2022
;
Ma and Ying, 2022
;
Lu, 2023
;
Kim et al., 2024
;
Lascu et al., 2023
;
Lascu et al., 2024a
;
Lascu et al., 2024b
;
Ding et al., 2024
;
Conger et al., 2024
;
An and Lu, 2025
;
Lu and Monmarché, 2025
)
.
Our main motivation in this work comes from the open problem by
Wang and Chizat (2024)
, who pose the question of studying the convergence guarantees of the simple mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics and its particle approximations for zero-sum games in the space of distributions, in particular without using two timescales or modifying the dynamics or algorithm.

The question posed by
Wang and Chizat (2024)
is for a general setting on a manifold, without convexity assumption on the interaction function.
In this work, we contribute an answer to this question for the simple case on the unconstrained Euclidean space with a strong convexity assumption on the base interaction function.
From the perspective of optimization on the space of probability distributions under the Wasserstein metric, this makes the payoff function
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
in (
4
) a strongly convex-strongly concave function, so it is natural to expect that the min-max gradient flow in the space of distributions—which is exactly the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics (
7
)—to converge exponentially fast.
However, precise results in this simple setting taking into account particle approximation and time discretization seem to be previously unknown.
We establish these results in this paper to provide a baseline toward understanding the more general question posed by
Wang and Chizat (2024)
.

We review a few works that are the most related to our work; see also
(
Wang and Chizat, 2024
)
and the references therein.

Domingo-Enrich et al. (2020)
study the setting when the domains are compact Riemannian manifolds, and show a conditional convergence result that if the mean-field dynamics converges, then it converges to the equilibrium distribution of the game.

Ma and Ying (2022)
and
Lu (2023)
also study the compact manifold setting with two-timescale dynamics.

Ma and Ying (2022)
study the quasistatic regime where one player is always at optimality, and establish the asymptotic convergence of the mean-field dynamics to the equilibrium distribution; they also study the finite-particle and discrete-time approximation, without convergence analysis.

Lu (2023)
study the two-timescale dynamics with finite timescale separation, and establish the exponential convergence guarantees of the mean-field dynamics to the equilibrium distribution.
In the compact manifold setting, only smoothness assumptions on the interaction function are needed.

When the domains are Euclidean spaces, some convexity or integrability assumptions are needed.

Kim et al. (2024)
consider a more general setting of zero-sum games with entropy regularization where the interaction functional on the space of distributions is convex-concave (whereas in our setting (
3
) the interaction functional is bilinear since it is an expectation over a base function);
they study a modified mean-field Langevin averaged-gradient dynamics where the drift term uses a time average of the gradients over the iterates, and show a continuous-time convergence rate, as well as a convergence analysis of the finite-particle discrete-time algorithm.

Ding et al. (2024)
consider the setting when the base interaction function is a bounded perturbation of a quadratic function;
they study a finite-particle discrete-time algorithm that implements the mirror-prox primal-dual algorithm in the space of distributions, which requires an inner loop running a sampling algorithm to implement each iteration, and show explicit convergence guarantees of the resulting algorithm.

The work of
Conger et al. (2024)
study a very general setting of coupled Wasserstein gradient flows for min-max and cooperative games in the space of distributions, with payoff functionals which include additional interaction energy terms, which are not present in our setting.
They show convergence guarantees of the mean-field dynamics in continuous time under convexity assumptions, utilizing the convexity structure in the Wasserstein space of distributions.
A special setting of their result
(
Conger et al., 2024
, Theorem 3.4)
, for zero-sum games with a bilinear interaction functional as in (
3
) with a strongly convex-strongly concave base interaction function, already shows the exponential convergence guarantee of the mean-field dynamics to the equilibrium distribution, as in our Theorem
3
, and thus they have answered the open question by
Wang and Chizat (2024)
for the mean-field setting with strongly convex interaction.
However,
Conger et al. (2024)
focuses on the continuous-time mean-field analysis, and in this work we complement the results by considering finite-particle approximation and discrete-time analysis.

Appendix B
Proofs for the Properties of Equilibrium Distribution and Duality Gap

B.1
Proof of Lemma
1
(Properties of the Best-Response Distribution)

Proof.

We note the KL divergence between two distributions
\rho,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be decomposed as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho\,\|\,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho\,\log\rho\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho\,\log\nu\,dx=-H(\rho)+\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[-\log\nu]

where
H
is the negative entropy functional.
For all
\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, from the definitions (
10
) of the
distributions
\nu^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\rho^{Y})
and
\nu^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\rho^{X})
, we have:

\displaystyle-\log\nu^{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{Y}}[V(x,Y)]+\log C^{Y}(\rho^{Y}),

(21a)

\displaystyle-\log\nu^{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}[V(X,y)]+\log C^{X}(\rho^{X})

(21b)

where
C^{Y}(\rho^{Y})
and
C^{X}(\rho^{X})
are the normalizing constants:

\displaystyle C^{Y}(\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{Y}}[V(x,Y)]\right)\,dx,

(22a)

\displaystyle C^{X}(\rho^{X})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}[V(X,y)]\right)\,dy.

(22b)

Then from the definition (
4
) of the payoff function
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}
, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\rho^{Y}}[V(X,Y)]-\tau H(\rho^{X})+\tau H(\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=\tau\left(-H(\rho^{X})+\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{Y}}\left[V(X,Y)\right]\right]\right)+\tau H(\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=\tau\left(-H(\rho^{X})+\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[-\log\nu^{X}\right]-\log C^{Y}(\rho^{Y})\right)+\tau H(\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\nu^{X})-\tau\log C^{Y}(\rho^{Y})+\tau H(\rho^{Y}).

(23)

Then as a function of
\rho^{X}
, we see that
\rho^{X}\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})
is minimized when we set
\rho^{X}=\nu^{X}
:

\nu^{X}=\arg\min_{\tilde{\rho}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}^{X},\rho^{Y}).

Similarly, we can also write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=-\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\nu^{Y})+\tau\log C^{X}(\rho^{X})-\tau H(\rho^{X}).

(24)

Then as a function of
\rho^{Y}
, we see that
\rho^{Y}\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})
is maximized when we set
\rho^{Y}=\nu^{Y}
:

\nu^{Y}=\arg\max_{\tilde{\rho}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\tilde{\rho}^{Y}).

The above computation also gives us:

\displaystyle\min_{\tilde{\rho}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}^{X},\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=-\tau\log C^{Y}(\rho^{Y})+\tau H(\rho^{Y})=\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})-\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\nu^{X})

\displaystyle\max_{\tilde{\rho}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\tilde{\rho}^{Y})

\displaystyle=\;\tau\log C^{X}(\rho^{X})-\tau H(\rho^{X})\;=\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})+\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\nu^{Y}).

Therefore, we can write the duality gap as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DG}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})=\max_{\tilde{\rho}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\tilde{\rho}^{Y})-\min_{\tilde{\rho}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}^{X},\rho^{Y})=\tau\left(\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\nu^{X})+\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\nu^{Y})\right)

as desired.
∎

B.2
Proof of Lemma
2
(Existence, Uniqueness of Equilibrium and Bound on Duality Gap)

Proof of Lemma
2
.

(1) Existence of equilibrium distribution:

Suppose
\bar{Z}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
evolves following the mean-field dynamics (
12
) from
\bar{Z}_{0}\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, and let
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}
where
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y})
and
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X})
are the best-response distributions.
We show in Lemma
10
that
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
for all
t\geq 0
.
In particular,
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
is also in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, the space of probability distributions over
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with finite second moment (without requiring absolute continuity with respect to the Lebesgue measure).

Furthermore, we show in Theorem
3
that for all
t\geq 0
:

\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}).

We claim this implies
(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z})_{t\geq 0}
is a (continuous-time) Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
with the
W_{2}
metric.
Since
\bar{Z}_{t}
evolves following the dynamics (
12
),

\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
evolves following the Fokker-Planck equation:

\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\partial t}=\tau\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\nabla\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right)
,
so its Wasserstein speed (norm of the velocity) is:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\partial t}\right\|_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}=\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right\|^{2}\right]^{1/2}=\tau\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})^{1/2}\leq e^{-\alpha t}\,\tau\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})^{1/2}.

Then using the definition of the Wasserstein distance as the shortest path length, we can estimate:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\bar{\rho}_{T_{0}}^{Z},\bar{\rho}_{T_{1}}^{Z})\,\leq\,\int_{T_{0}}^{T_{1}}\left\|\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\partial t}\right\|_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\,dt\,\leq\,\tau\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})^{1/2}\int_{T_{0}}^{T_{1}}e^{-\alpha t}\,dt\,\leq\,e^{-\alpha T_{0}}\,\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})^{1/2}.

Therefore, for any
T_{1}>T_{0}
, we have
W_{2}(\bar{\rho}_{T_{0}}^{Z},\bar{\rho}_{T_{1}}^{Z})\to 0
as
T_{0}\to\infty
.
This shows that
(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z})_{t\geq 0}
is a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
with the
W_{2}
metric.
Since
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
with the
W_{2}
metric is a complete metric space
(
Villani, 2009
, Theorem 6.18)
, this implies
(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z})_{t\geq 0}
must converge to a limit:
\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Z}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
which is also in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.
Since each
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
is a product distribution, the limit
\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y}
must also be a product distribution.

Furthermore, since
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})=0
, the limiting distribution
\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Z}
is a fixed point for the best-response map, i.e.,
\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y}
satisfies:

\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y}),\qquad\qquad\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X}).

Therefore,
\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Z}
minimizes the duality gap:
\mathsf{DG}(\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X},\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y})=0
, and thus,
(\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X},\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y})
is an equilibrium distribution for the game (
3
), as defined in (
5
).
Furthermore, from the definition of the best-response maps (
10
), we see that
\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\rho_{\infty}^{Y})
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, since we assume
V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly convex in
x
.
Similarly,
\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\rho_{\infty}^{X})
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, since we assume
V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly concave in
y
.

(2) Bound on duality gap:

Let
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be an equilibrium distribution for the game (
3
), which exists by the argument above (which we call
(\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{X},\bar{\rho}_{\infty}^{Y})
above).
Since
\bar{\nu}^{X}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, it also satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-Talagrand inequality, which means for all
\rho^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})\geq\frac{\tau}{2\alpha}\,W_{2}(\rho^{X},\bar{\nu}^{X})^{2}.

Similarly, since
\bar{\nu}^{Y}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, it satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-Talagrand inequality, so for all
\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y})\geq\frac{\tau}{2\alpha}\,W_{2}(\rho^{Y},\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{2}.

Adding the two bounds above gives the first inequality claimed in (
11
).

Next, since
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})
is a fixed point of the best-response map, the identities from (
21
) become:

\displaystyle-\log\bar{\nu}^{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[V(x,Y)]+\log C^{Y}(\bar{\nu}^{Y}),

\displaystyle-\log\bar{\nu}^{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}[V(X,y)]+\log C^{X}(\bar{\nu}^{X})

where
C^{X},C^{Y}
are the normalizing constants defined in (
22
).
Then we can compute:

\displaystyle H(\bar{\nu}^{X})=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}\left[-\log\bar{\nu}^{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[V(X,Y)]+\log C^{Y}(\bar{\nu}^{Y})

\displaystyle H(\bar{\nu}^{Y})=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[-\log\bar{\nu}^{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[V(X,Y)]+\log C^{X}(\bar{\nu}^{X}).

Adding the two equations, we obtain:

\displaystyle H(\bar{\nu}^{X})+H(\bar{\nu}^{Y})=\log C^{Y}(\bar{\nu}^{Y})+\log C^{X}(\bar{\nu}^{X}).

(25)

Therefore, for all
\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, from the definition of the duality gap, and using the relations (
23
) and (
24
), the identities
\bar{\nu}^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\bar{\nu}^{Y})
,
\bar{\nu}^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\bar{\nu}^{X})
, and the identity (
25
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DG}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=\max_{\tilde{\rho}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\tilde{\rho}^{Y})-\min_{\tilde{\rho}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}^{X},\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})-\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\bar{\nu}^{X},\rho^{Y})

\displaystyle=\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})-\tau\log C^{Y}(\bar{\nu}^{Y})+\tau H(\bar{\nu}^{Y})

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(-\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y})+\tau\log C^{X}(\bar{\nu}^{X})-\tau H(\bar{\nu}^{X})\right)

\displaystyle=\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})+\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y})

which is the second inequality claimed in (
11
).

(3) Uniqueness of equilibrium distribution:

Suppose the contrary that we have two equilibrium distributions
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})
and
(\bar{\mu}^{X},\bar{\mu}^{Y})
of the game (
3
).
Then by using the second inequality in the bound (
11
) with
(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})=(\bar{\mu}^{X},\bar{\mu}^{Y})
, we have:

\displaystyle\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\mu}^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})+\tau\,\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\mu}^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y})\leq\mathsf{DG}(\bar{\mu}^{X},\bar{\mu}^{Y})=0

where the last equality follows because
(\bar{\mu}^{X},\bar{\mu}^{Y})
is an equilibrium distribution so it minimizes the duality gap.
Therefore, we must have
\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\mu}^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})=0
, so
\bar{\mu}^{X}=\bar{\nu}^{X}
.
Similarly, we must have
\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\mu}^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y})=0
, so
\bar{\mu}^{Y}=\bar{\nu}^{Y}
.
Therefore, the equilibrium distribution of the game (
3
) is unique.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for the Mean-Field Min-Max Langevin Dynamics

C.1
Derivation of the Mean-Field Dynamics

At an idealized continuous-time level, a natural strategy to compute the equilibrium distribution of the game (
3
) is for each player to run the gradient flow dynamics in the space of probability distributions to minimize their own objective function.
Suppose we endow the space of probability distribution
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the Wasserstein
W_{2}
metric.
Then each player maintains a continuous-time curve of probability distributions
(\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, which they evolve via:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{grad}_{\rho^{X}}\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t},\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t}),\qquad\qquad\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t}}{\partial t}=\mathrm{grad}_{\rho^{Y}}\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t},\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t}).

In the above,
\mathrm{grad}_{\rho^{X}}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})
denotes the Wasserstein gradient of
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})
with respect to the first argument
\rho^{X}
, with the second argument
\rho^{Y}
fixed.
Similarly,
\mathrm{grad}_{\rho^{Y}}\mathcal{F}_{\tau}(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})
denotes the Wasserstein gradient with respect to the second argument
\rho^{Y}
, with the first argument
\rho^{X}
fixed.

Following the computation rule for the Wasserstein gradient
(
Villani, 2009
)
, the above system of gradient flow dynamics corresponds to the following system of Fokker-Planck equations:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t}}[\nabla_{x}V(\cdot,\bar{Y}_{t})]\right)+\tau\Delta\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}^{X}_{t}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X}_{t},\cdot)]\right)+\tau\Delta\bar{\rho}^{Y}_{t}.

In the above,
\nabla_{x}V(x,y)
and
\nabla_{y}V(x,y)
denote the gradient with respect to the first and second argument, respectively, while keeping the other argument fixed.
Here
\nabla\cdot
is the divergence (trace of the Jacobian) of a vector field, and
\Delta
is the Laplacian (trace of the Hessian) of a function.

The above system of Fokker-Planck equations can be realized as the continuity equations for a pair of stochastic processes
(\bar{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\bar{Y}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which evolve following the
mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics
:

\displaystyle d\bar{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})]\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{X}

\displaystyle d\bar{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})]\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{Y}

where
(W_{t}^{X})_{t\geq 0}
and
(W_{t}^{Y})_{t\geq 0}
are independent standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The above is a mean-field system because the evolution of
\bar{X}_{t}
depends on the distribution
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
of the other player, while the evolution of
\bar{Y}_{t}
depends on the distribution
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}
.
However, note the dependence is only via their expectations, so in the mean-field system above,
\bar{X}_{t}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}
evolve independently, i.e., they do not interact at the level of random variables, only at the level of distributions.
In particular, it does not matter that we use independent Brownian motions for
\bar{X}_{t}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}
, we could also use the same Brownian motion; we leave it as the above for convenience when writing the dynamics in the joint form in Section
4
.

C.2
Time Derivative of Relative Fisher Information along Mean-Field Langevin Dynamics

We show the following identity on the time derivative of the relative Fisher information between the iterate of the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics and its best-response distribution.
In Lemma
9
below, the first term on the right-hand side is the second-order relative Fisher information between
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
and
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}
, and the second term is a weighted relative Fisher information.

We note the identity in Lemma
9
is formally identical to the time derivative identity of the relative Fisher information
\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\nu)
(or the second derivative of the KL divergence
\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\nu)
) when
\rho_{t}
evolves along the Langevin dynamics to the target distribution
\nu
, using the Otto calculus formula for the Hessian of KL divergence; see
(
Villani, 2009
, Formula 15.7)
or
(
Wibisono and Jog, 2018
, Eq. (10) in Appendix A)
.
Since the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics is the min-max Wasserstein gradient flow in the space of distributions, the identity in Lemma
9
can also be formally derived via Otto calculus computation.
We provide a proof of Lemma
9
via an explicit computation below.
See
(
Conger et al., 2024
, Lemma 7.2)
for an alternative proof for a similar result, and see also the proof of Theorem
35
part (4) in Section
H.1
for the finite-dimensional version of this identity.

We denote
\|u\|^{2}_{A}:=u^{\top}Au
and
\|A\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}:=\mathsf{Tr}(A^{\top}A)
for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}
.

Lemma 9
.

Suppose
\bar{Z}_{t}=(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
evolves following the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics (
12
) in the joint space
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Define its best-response distribution
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}
where
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y})
and
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X})
as defined in (
10
).
Then for all
t\geq 0
, we have the identity:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})

\displaystyle=-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right\|^{2}_{(-\nabla^{2}\log\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})}\right].

Proof.

For ease of notation, in this proof we write
\rho_{t}^{Z}\equiv\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
and
\nu_{t}^{Z}\equiv\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}
, i.e., we drop the superscript bar notation (which denotes distributions along mean-field dynamics).
Similarly, we write
\rho_{t}^{X}\equiv\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}
,
\rho_{t}^{Y}\equiv\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
, and
\nu_{t}^{X}\equiv\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}
,
\nu_{t}^{Y}\equiv\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}
.

We define
f_{t}=-\log\rho_{t}^{Z}
and
g_{t}=-\log\nu_{t}^{Z}
, so
f_{t},g_{t}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}
are separable functions, in particular:

g_{t}(z)=g_{t}(x,y)=g_{t}^{X}(x)+g_{t}^{Y}(y)

where
g_{t}^{X}=-\log\nu_{t}^{X}
and
g_{t}^{Y}=-\log\nu_{t}^{Y}
.
Recall from the definition (
10
) for
\nu_{t}^{X}=\Phi^{X}(\rho_{t}^{Y})
and
\nu_{t}^{Y}=\Phi^{Y}(\rho_{t}^{X})
, we have:

\displaystyle\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(x,Y)]

(26a)

\displaystyle\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(X,y)].

(26b)

This also implies:

\displaystyle\Delta g_{t}^{X}(x)

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Y}}\left[\Delta_{x}V(x,Y)\right]

(27a)

\displaystyle\Delta g_{t}^{Y}(y)

\displaystyle=-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{X}}\left[\Delta_{y}V(X,y)\right].

(27b)

The mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics (
12
) can be written as the SDE:

\displaystyle d\bar{Z}_{t}\,=\,-\tau\nabla g_{t}(\bar{Z}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}

Then
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\exp(-f_{t})
evolves following the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{Z}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\tau\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{Z}\nabla g_{t}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z}.

(28)

In particular, for the components
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
, we also have:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{X}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\tau\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{X}\nabla g_{t}^{X}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{X}

(29a)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{Y}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\tau\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{Y}\nabla g_{t}^{Y}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{Y}.

(29b)

We will use the following relations for
f_{t}=-\log\rho_{t}^{Z}
:

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\nabla f_{t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\rho_{t}^{Z}

(30a)

\displaystyle\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\nabla^{2}f_{t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{2}\rho_{t}^{Z}+\rho_{t}^{Z}(\nabla f_{t})(\nabla f_{t})^{\top}

(30b)

\displaystyle\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z}

\displaystyle=-\rho_{t}^{Z}\Delta f_{t}+\rho_{t}^{Z}\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}.

(30c)

Then we can compute that
f_{t}=-\log\rho_{t}^{Z}
evolves following the equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f_{t}=-\frac{1}{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{Z}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left(\tau\langle\nabla\rho_{t}^{Z},\nabla g_{t}\rangle+\tau\rho_{t}^{Z}\Delta g_{t}+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla g_{t}\rangle-\tau\Delta g_{t}+\tau\Delta f_{t}-\tau\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}

(31)

We will also use the
Bochner’s formula
, which states that for smooth
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla u,\nabla\Delta u\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\Delta\|\nabla u\|^{2}-\|\nabla^{2}u\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}.

(32)

By integration by parts, we can write the relative Fisher information as:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\nu_{t}^{Z})\,

\displaystyle=\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla f_{t}-\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}+\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}-2\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla g_{t}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\right]}}{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\,+\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\right]}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\,-\,2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\Delta g_{t}\right]}}.

(33)

We compute the time derivative of the three terms above separately, with explicit computation below, color-coded for clarity for when we combine them.

(I) First term:

The first term in (
33
) is the Fisher information of
\rho_{t}^{Z}
.
We compute its time derivative using the Fokker-Planck equation (
28
) and the formula (
31
) to get:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{Z})\,\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\,dz+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla\partial_{t}f_{t}\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}^{Z}\nabla g_{t})\,\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\,dz+\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z})\,\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\,dz

(34a)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla(\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla g_{t}\rangle-\Delta g_{t})\rangle\,dz

(34b)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla(\Delta f_{t})\rangle\,dz-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\rangle\,dz.

(34c)

We calculate the terms above one by one.

1.

The first term in (
34a
) is, by integration by parts:

\displaystyle\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}^{Z}\nabla g_{t})\,\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=-\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\langle\nabla g_{t},\,\nabla(\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2})\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\langle\nabla g_{t},\,(\nabla^{2}f_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\rangle\,dz.

2.

The second term in (
34a
) is, by integration by parts:

\displaystyle\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z})\,\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\,dz=\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\Delta\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\,dz.

3.

The term in (
34b
) is, by distributing the gradient,

\displaystyle 2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla(\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla g_{t}\rangle-\Delta g_{t})\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\left(\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}f_{t})\,\nabla g_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\rangle-\langle\nabla f_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle\right)dz.

4.

The first term in (
34c
) is, by Bochner’s formula (
32
):

\displaystyle 2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla(\Delta f_{t})\rangle\,dz
