\displaystyle=\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\Delta\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\,dz-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\|\nabla^{2}f_{t}\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\,dz.

5.

The second term in (
34c
) is, using relation (
30b
) and integration by parts:

\displaystyle-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\nabla\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\langle\nabla\rho_{t}^{Z},\nabla\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\rangle\,dz=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\Delta\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}dz.

Combining the above, we see that the terms involving
\langle\nabla g_{t},\,(\nabla^{2}f_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\rangle
and
\Delta\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}
vanish, so we are left with:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\right]=-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]+2\tau\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\rangle\right]-2\tau\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle\right].}}

(35)

(II) Second term:

For the second term in (
33
), using the Fokker-Planck equation (
28
), we have:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{Z})\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\,dz+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla g_{t},\partial_{t}(\nabla g_{t})\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}^{Z}\nabla g_{t})\,\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\,dz\,+\,\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\,dz

(36a)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}\,\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\partial_{t}(\nabla g_{t}^{X})\rangle\,dx+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}\,\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\partial_{t}(\nabla g_{t}^{Y})\rangle\,dy

(36b)

where in the second line above we have used the fact that
g_{t}(x,y)=g_{t}^{X}(x)+g_{t}^{Y}(y)
is separable, and
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
is independent.
We calculate the terms above one by one.

1.

The first term in (
36a
) is, by integration by parts:

\displaystyle\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}^{Z}\nabla g_{t})\,\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=-\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\langle\nabla g_{t},\nabla(\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2})\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\langle\nabla g_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla g_{t}\rangle\,dz.

2.

The second term in (
36a
) is, by integration by parts and using relation (
30a
):

\displaystyle\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\,dz

\displaystyle=\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\langle\nabla f_{t},\,\nabla\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\langle\nabla f_{t},\,(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla g_{t}\rangle\,dz.

3.

For the first term in (
36b
), we can compute using (
26a
) and (
29b
), and integration by parts:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x))

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{Y}\nabla g_{t}^{Y}\right)(y)+\Delta\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\right)\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\,dy

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\,\nabla^{2}_{yx}V(x,y)\,\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y)\,dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\Delta_{y}\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\,dy.

Then, since
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
, we can compute:

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}\,\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\partial_{t}(\nabla g_{t}^{X})\rangle\,dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\,\left\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x),\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\left(-\nabla^{2}_{yx}V(x,y)\,\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y)+\Delta_{y}\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\right)dy\right\rangle\,dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}(x,y)\,\left\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x),\,-\nabla^{2}_{yx}V(x,y)\,\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y)+\Delta_{y}\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\right\rangle\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\left\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\,-(\nabla^{2}_{yx}V)\nabla g_{t}^{Y}+\Delta_{y}\nabla_{x}V\right\rangle\,dz.

4.

For the second term in (
36b
), we can compute using (
26b
) and (
29a
), and integration by parts:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y))

\displaystyle=-\tau^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\,dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{X}\nabla g_{t}^{X}\right)(x)+\Delta\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\right)\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\,\nabla^{2}_{xy}V(x,y)\,\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x)\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\,dx.

Then, since
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
, we can compute:

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}\,\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\partial_{t}(\nabla g_{t}^{Y})\rangle\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\,\left\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y),\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\left(\nabla^{2}_{xy}V(x,y)\,\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x)-\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\right)dx\right\rangle\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}(x,y)\,\left\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y),\,\nabla^{2}_{xy}V(x,y)\,\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x)-\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\right\rangle\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\left\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\,(\nabla^{2}_{xy}V)\nabla g_{t}^{X}-\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}V\right\rangle\,dz.

Since
\nabla^{2}_{xy}V=(\nabla^{2}_{yx}V)^{\top}
, when we combine the calculations above, we see that the terms involving
\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\nabla^{2}_{xy}V\;\nabla g_{t}^{X}\rangle
cancel, so we get:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\right]}}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\,=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\tau\,\langle\nabla f_{t}-\nabla g_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\nabla g_{t}\rangle+2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\Delta_{y}\nabla_{x}V\rangle-2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}V\rangle\right].}}

(37)

(III) Third term:

For the third term in (
33
), using the Fokker-Planck equation (
28
), we have:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(-2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\Delta g_{t}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{Z})\,\Delta g_{t}\,dz-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\partial_{t}(\Delta g_{t})\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}^{Z}\nabla g_{t})\,(\Delta g_{t})\,dz-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z})\,(\Delta g_{t})\,dz

(38a)

\displaystyle\qquad-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}\,\partial_{t}(\Delta_{x}g_{t}^{X})\,dx-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}\,\partial_{t}(\Delta_{y}g_{t}^{Y})\,dy

(38b)

where in the second line above we have used the fact that
g_{t}(x,y)=g_{t}^{X}(x)+g_{t}^{Y}(y)
is separable, and
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
is independent.
We calculate the terms above one by one.

1.

The first term in (
38a
) is, by integration by parts:

\displaystyle-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}^{Z}\nabla g_{t})\,(\Delta g_{t})\,dz

\displaystyle=2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\langle\nabla g_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle\,dz.

2.

The second term in (
38a
) is, by integration by parts and denoting
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{2d})
:

\displaystyle-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(\Delta\rho_{t}^{Z})\,(\Delta g_{t})\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{i}^{2}}\rho_{t}^{Z}(z)\right)\,\left(\sum_{j=1}^{2d}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{j}^{2}}g_{t}(z)\right)\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2d}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{i}^{2}}\rho_{t}^{Z}(z)\right)\,\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{j}^{2}}g_{t}(z)\right)\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2d}\rho_{t}^{Z}(z)\,\left(\frac{\partial^{4}}{\partial z_{i}^{2}\,\partial z_{j}^{2}}g_{t}(z)\right)\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{2d}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{i}\,\partial z_{j}}\rho_{t}^{Z}(z)\right)\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z_{i}\,\partial z_{j}}g_{t}(z)\right)\,dz

\displaystyle=-2\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\langle\nabla^{2}\rho_{t}^{Z},\nabla^{2}g_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}\,dz

\displaystyle=2\tau\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\left(\langle\nabla^{2}f_{t},\nabla^{2}g_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}-\left\langle\nabla f_{t},\,(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\right\rangle\right)\,dz

where in the last step we have used relation (
30b
).

3.

For the first term in (
38b
), we can compute using (
27a
) and (
29b
), and integration by parts:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\Delta_{x}g_{t}^{X}(x))

\displaystyle=\tau^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\,\Delta_{x}V(x,y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{Y}\nabla g_{t}^{Y}\right)(y)+\Delta\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\right)\,\Delta_{x}V(x,y)\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\left(-\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y),\nabla_{y}\Delta_{x}V(x,y)\rangle\,+\,\Delta_{y}\Delta_{x}V(x,y)\right)dy.

Therefore, since
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
:

\displaystyle-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}\,\partial_{t}(\Delta_{x}g_{t}^{X})\,dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\left(\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y}(y),\nabla_{y}\Delta_{x}V(x,y)\rangle\,-\,\Delta_{y}\Delta_{x}V(x,y)\right)dy\,dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\left(\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\nabla_{y}\Delta_{x}V\rangle\,-\,\Delta_{y}\Delta_{x}V\right)dz.

4.

For the second term in (
38b
), we can compute using (
27b
) and (
29a
), and integration by parts:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\Delta g_{t}^{Y}(y))

\displaystyle=-\tau^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{t}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\,\Delta_{y}V(x,y)\,dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{X}\nabla g_{t}^{X}\right)(x)+\Delta\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\right)\,\Delta_{y}V(x,y)\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\left(\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x),\nabla_{x}\Delta_{y}V(x,y)\rangle\,-\,\Delta_{x}\Delta_{y}V(x,y)\right)dx.

Therefore, since
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
:

\displaystyle-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}\,\partial_{t}(\Delta_{y}g_{t}^{Y})\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{Y}(y)\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\rho_{t}^{X}(x)\left(-\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X}(x),\nabla_{x}\Delta_{y}V(x,y)\rangle\,+\,\Delta_{x}\Delta_{y}V(x,y)\right)dx\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\left(-\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\nabla_{x}\Delta_{y}V\rangle\,+\,\Delta_{x}\Delta_{y}V\right)dz.

When we combine the calculations above, we see that the terms involving
\Delta_{x}\Delta_{y}V=\Delta_{y}\Delta_{x}V
cancel, so we get:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\frac{d}{dt}\left(-2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\Delta g_{t}\right]\right)}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\,=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\tau\langle\nabla g_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle+2\tau\langle\nabla^{2}f_{t},\nabla^{2}g_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}-2\tau\left\langle\nabla f_{t},\,(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\right\rangle\right]}}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\,+\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\nabla_{y}\Delta_{x}V\rangle\,-\,2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\nabla_{x}\Delta_{y}V\rangle\right].}}

(39)

Combining the terms.

Combining the calculations in (
35
), (
37
), and (
39
) below,
we see that the terms involving
\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\Delta_{y}\nabla_{x}V\rangle
,
\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}V\rangle
,
and
\left\langle\nabla f_{t},\,(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\right\rangle
cancel.
Then we can rearrange the remaining terms to get:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\nu_{t}^{Z})

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\right]}}+{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\|\nabla g_{t}\|^{2}\right]}}+{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\frac{d}{dt}\left(-2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\Delta g_{t}\right]\right)}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]+2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\rangle\right]-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle\right]}}

\displaystyle\qquad{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\,+\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\tau\,\langle\nabla f_{t}-\nabla g_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\nabla g_{t}\rangle+2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\Delta_{y}\nabla_{x}V\rangle-2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}V\rangle\right]}}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\,+\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\tau\langle\nabla g_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle+2\tau\langle\nabla^{2}f_{t},\nabla^{2}g_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}-2\tau\left\langle\nabla f_{t},\,(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\right\rangle\right]}}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\,+\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{Y},\nabla_{y}\Delta_{x}V\rangle\,-\,2\langle\nabla g_{t}^{X},\nabla_{x}\Delta_{y}V\rangle\right]}}

\displaystyle={{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle\right]}}

\displaystyle\qquad{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\,+\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\tau\,\langle\nabla f_{t}-\nabla g_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\nabla g_{t}\rangle\right]}}

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}{\,+\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[2\tau\langle\nabla g_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle+2\tau\langle\nabla^{2}f_{t},\nabla^{2}g_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]}}.

We then complete the squares to form the first two terms below, which are the good terms that we want, and collect the rest in a remainder term:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\nu_{t}^{Z})

\displaystyle=-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{t}-\nabla^{2}g_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla f_{t}-\nabla g_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\nabla^{2}g_{t}}\right]+2\tau R(t)

where we define the remainder term
R(t)
as the difference of the two sides above (scaled by
2\tau
):

\displaystyle R(t)

\displaystyle:=-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla^{2}f_{t},\nabla^{2}g_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}g_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\nabla g_{t}\rangle\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\nabla f_{t}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla g_{t},\,\nabla\Delta g_{t}\rangle\right]

We claim this remainder term is identically zero.
Indeed, by relation (
30a
) and integration by parts, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\nabla f_{t}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\langle\nabla\rho_{t}^{Z},\,(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla f_{t}\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\left(\langle\nabla\Delta g_{t},\nabla f_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{2}g_{t},\nabla^{2}f_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}\right)\,dz

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla\Delta g_{t},\nabla f_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{2}g_{t},\nabla^{2}f_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}\right].

where in the above we have used the identity
\nabla\cdot\nabla^{2}g_{t}=\nabla\Delta g_{t}
.
Similarly:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla f_{t},(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\nabla g_{t}\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\langle\nabla\rho_{t}^{Z},\,(\nabla^{2}g_{t})\,\nabla g_{t}\rangle\,dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\rho_{t}^{Z}\left(\langle\nabla\Delta g_{t},\nabla g_{t}\rangle+\langle\nabla^{2}g_{t},\nabla^{2}g_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{HS}}\right)\,dz

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\langle\nabla\Delta g_{t},\nabla g_{t}\rangle+\left\|\nabla^{2}g_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right].

Therefore, we see that all the terms in the remainder term cancel, so indeed
R(t)=0
.
Thus, we have shown the identity:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\nu_{t}^{Z})

\displaystyle=-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}f_{t}-\nabla^{2}g_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla f_{t}-\nabla g_{t}\right\|^{2}_{\nabla^{2}g_{t}}\right].

∎

C.3
Bound on the Second Moment Along the Mean-Field Dynamics

We show that along the mean-field dynamics (
7
), the distributions remain in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.

Lemma 10
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\bar{Z}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
evolves following the mean-field min-max Langevin dynamics (
7
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
from
\bar{Z}_{0}\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.
Then
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

We note that
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by virtue of the Brownian motion component in the dynamics (
7
).
We now show that the second moment of
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}
remains finite for all
t\geq 0
.

Under Assumption
1
, recall from Theorem
35
(in Section
H
) that there exists a unique equilibrium point
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
which satisfies
\nabla V(z^{*})=0
.
Define the vector field
b^{Z}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by, for all
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
:

\displaystyle b^{Z}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\\
\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\end{pmatrix}.

Observe that
b^{Z}
is the case
N=1
of the vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
that we defined in (
17
) (in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}=\mathbb{R}^{2d}
).
Note
b^{Z}(z^{*})=0
, since
\nabla_{x}V(z^{*})=\nabla_{y}V(z^{*})=0
. Lemma
12
in the next section guarantees that
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.

Since
\bar{Z}_{t}=(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})
evolves following the mean-field dynamics (
7
), its density
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}
evolves following the Fokker-Planck equations:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(\cdot,\bar{Y}_{t})]\right)+\tau\,\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X}_{t},\cdot)]\right)+\tau\,\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}.

Then we can compute, using integration by parts:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}}\left[\left\|\bar{X}_{t}-x^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}(x)}{\partial t}\,\|x-x^{*}\|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(\cdot,\bar{Y}_{t})]\right)(x)+\tau\,\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}(x)\right)\|x-x^{*}\|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}(x)\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(x,\bar{Y}_{t})],\nabla\left(\|x-x^{*}\|^{2}\right)\right\rangle dx+\tau\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}(x)\Delta\left(\|x-x^{*}\|^{2}\right)dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}(x)\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(x,\bar{Y}_{t})],x-x^{*}\right\rangle\,dx+2\tau d

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{X}}\left[\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t})],X_{t}-x^{*}\right\rangle\right]+2\tau d

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\langle\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t}),\bar{X}_{t}-x^{*}\right\rangle\right]+2\tau d.

Similarly, we can also compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\bar{Y}_{t}-y^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\langle\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t}),\bar{Y}_{t}-y^{*}\right\rangle\right]+2\tau d.

Adding the two identities above gives:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\langle\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t}),\bar{X}_{t}-x^{*}\right\rangle\right]+2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\langle\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X}_{t},\bar{Y}_{t}),\bar{Y}_{t}-y^{*}\right\rangle\right]+4\tau d

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\langle b^{Z}(\bar{Z}_{t}),\bar{Z}_{t}-z^{*}\right\rangle\right]+4\tau d

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\langle b^{Z}(\bar{Z}_{t})-b^{Z}(z^{*}),\bar{Z}_{t}-z^{*}\right\rangle\right]+4\tau d

\displaystyle\leq-2\alpha\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+4\tau d

where the inequality follows from the property that
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
We can write the differential inequality above equivalently as:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{2\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\right)\leq e^{2\alpha t}\,4\tau d.

Integrating from
0
to
t
and rearranging the result gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{0}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{(1-e^{-2\alpha t})}{\alpha}\,2\tau d

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{0}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2\tau d}{\alpha}.

Since
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
,
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]<\infty
, so
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{0}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]+2\|z^{*}\|^{2}<\infty
.
Therefore, we also have for all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+2\|z^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}_{0}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{4\tau d}{\alpha}+2\|z^{*}\|^{2}\,<\,\infty

which shows that
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.
∎

C.4
Proof of Theorem
3
(Convergence of the Mean-Field Min-Max Langevin Dynamics)

Proof of Theorem
3
.

By Assumption
1
, both
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}
and
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}
are
(\alpha/\tau)
-strongly log-concave, which can be directly verified from the form of their log-density functions.
Then
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}_{t}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Y}
is also
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, i.e.,

-\nabla^{2}\log\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}\succeq(\alpha/\tau)I.

Then by the identity from Lemma
9
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})

\displaystyle=-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla^{2}\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right\|^{2}_{\mathsf{HS}}\right]-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right\|^{2}_{(-\nabla^{2}\log\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq-2\tau\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right\|^{2}_{(-\nabla^{2}\log\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq-2\alpha\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}}{\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\alpha\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}).

In the first inequality above, we drop the first term in the identity, which is the second-order relative Fisher information.
In the second inequality, we use the property that
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC.
In the next step, we recognize the term in the previous line as also equal to the relative Fisher information.
Integrating the differential inequality above implies the desired convergence rate:

\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}).

Since
\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, it also satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-LSI.
Then by combining with the convergence rate for relative Fisher information above, we get:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})\leq\frac{\tau}{2\alpha}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{t}^{Z})\leq\frac{\tau}{2\alpha}\,e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}).

Multiplying both sides by
\tau
yields the desired convergence rate in duality gap.
∎

C.4.1
Bound on Relative Fisher Information to the Best-Response Distribution

We provide the following bound on the initial Fisher information
\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})
when
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}
is Gaussian and
\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}
is its best-response distribution.
Under Assumption
1
, recall from Theorem
35
(in Section
H
) that there exists a unique equilibrium point
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
which satisfies
\nabla V(z^{*})=0
,
and that
\nabla V
is
L
-Lipschitz.

Lemma 11
.

Assume Assumption
1
. Let
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}=\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\tau^{2}}{L^{2}}I_{d})
, and
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}=\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})\leq 2d\left(1+\frac{L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\right)+\frac{L^{2}\|z^{*}\|^{2}}{\tau^{2}}.

Proof.

Recall that the best-response distributions are defined by:

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{0}^{X}

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(-\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{Y}}[V(x,Y)]\right),

\displaystyle\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Y}

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}[V(X,y)]\right),

and
\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}_{0}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Y}
.
Since both
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}
and
\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z}
are product distributions,

\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})=\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})+\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y}).

Define
g^{X}=-\log\bar{\nu}_{0}^{X}
, so
\nabla g^{X}(x)=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(x,Y)]

and
\Delta g^{X}(x)=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}}[\Delta_{x}V(x,\bar{Y})]
.
Since we assume
V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly convex in
x
, we have
\Delta g^{X}(x)\geq\alpha d/\tau\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Note also that for
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\tau^{2}}{L^{2}}I)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{L^{4}}{\tau^{4}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}\left[\left\|X\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{L^{2}d}{\tau^{2}}.

Then by expanding the square and using integration by parts, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{X})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}+\nabla g^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]-2\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}\left[\Delta g^{X}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla g^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L^{2}d}{\tau^{2}}+\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}}\left[\left\|\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{Y\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(X,Y)]\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L^{2}d}{\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(X,Y)\right\|^{2}\right].

By an identical argument, we can similarly show:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Y})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L^{2}d}{\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{y}V(X,Y)\right\|^{2}\right].

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}d}{\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{X}\otimes\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(X,Y)\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla_{y}V(X,Y)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2Ld}{\tau}+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla V(Z)\right\|^{2}\right].

Using
\nabla V(z^{*})=0
and
\nabla V
is
L
-Lipschitz, and since
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}=\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\tau^{2}}{L^{2}}I_{2d})
, we can bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla V(Z)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla V(Z)-\nabla V(z^{*})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq L^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|Z-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]=2\tau^{2}d+L^{2}\|z^{*}\|^{2}.

Plugging this in to our earlier calculation above, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\bar{\rho}_{0}^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}_{0}^{Z})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}d}{\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\left(2\tau^{2}d+L^{2}\|z^{*}\|^{2}\right)=2d\left(1+\frac{L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\right)+\frac{L^{2}\|z^{*}\|^{2}}{\tau^{2}}.

∎

Appendix D
Technical Lemmas for the Finite-Particle Analysis

We collect some lemmas for our analysis of the finite-particle dynamics and algorithm.
Here we consider a particle discretization of the mean-field dynamics with
N
particles.
This means we work with a joint vector
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(x^{1},\dots,x^{N},y^{1},\dots,y^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, where each
x^{i},y^{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We recall some definitions.
Recall
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is the stationary distribution of the mean-field dynamics (
7
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Let
\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}[\|\bar{Z}-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}[\bar{Z}]\|^{2}]
be the variance of
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
.
Recall under Assumption
1
,
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, so we can bound the variance by
\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\leq\frac{2\tau d}{\alpha}
(see Lemma
24
in Section
F.5.1
), but in our computations below, we will keep it as the variance.

Recall we defined in (
18
) the tensorized power of the stationary mean-field distribution:

\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{X})^{\otimes N}\otimes(\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{\otimes N}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN}).

Recall we defined in (
17
) the vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, which is the drift term in the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) and the finite-particle algorithm (
20
).

Let us also define a vector field
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
by, for all
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\displaystyle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\\
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})\end{pmatrix}

(40)

where we define the vector fields
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{dN}
and
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{dN}
by, for all
\mathbf{x}=(x^{1},\dots,x^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
and
\mathbf{y}=(y^{1},\dots,y^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{dN}
:

\displaystyle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{b}^{X}(x^{1})\\
\cdots\\
\bar{b}^{X}(x^{N})\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[-\nabla_{x}V(x^{1},\bar{Y})]\\
\cdots\\
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[-\nabla_{x}V(x^{N},\bar{Y})]\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{b}^{Y}(y^{1})\\
\cdots\\
\bar{b}^{Y}(y^{N})\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X},y^{1})]\\
\cdots\\
\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X},y^{N})]\end{pmatrix}.

By the definition of
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
as the stationary distribution of the mean-field dynamics (
7
), so it is a fixed point for the best-response distribution, we observe that the vector field
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
can be written as a scaled version of the score function of the tensorized stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
:

\displaystyle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})=\tau\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}).

(41)

Finally, we also define the following stochastic process for
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
with drift term
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
:

\displaystyle d\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}=\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

(42)

where
W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
Observe that since
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\tau\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
,
the stationary distribution of the process (
42
) is equal to
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
Therefore, we call the process (
42
) the
tensorized mean-field dynamics
, since we can obtain it by replicating the base mean-field dynamics (
7
) from
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
for
N
times.
We will use the process (
42
) to compare the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) and algorithm (
20
).

D.1
Properties of the Vector Fields

D.1.1
Properties of the finite-particle vector field

Lemma 12
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then:

1.

The vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
17
) is
(2L)
-Lipschitz, which means for all
\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|\leq 2L\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\|.

2.

Furthermore,
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, which means for all
\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\left\langle b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}),\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rangle\leq-\alpha\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(x^{1},\dots,x^{N},y^{1},\dots,y^{N})

and
\bar{\mathbf{z}}=(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})=(\bar{x}^{1},\dots,\bar{x}^{N},\bar{y}^{1},\dots,\bar{y}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
be given.

(1)
We show
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(2L)
-Lipschitz.
Recall by assumption,
(x,y)\mapsto\nabla V(x,y)
is
L
-Lipschitz; in particular, each component
\nabla_{x}V(x,y)
and
\nabla_{y}V(x,y)
is also
L
-Lipschitz.
By definition,

\displaystyle\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-b^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})\right\|^{2}+\left\|b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})\right\|^{2}.

We bound each term above separately.
For the first term:

\displaystyle\left\|b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-b^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\|b^{X}(x^{i},\mathbf{y})-b^{X}(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{\mathbf{y}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\|-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},y^{j})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{y}^{j})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\|\sum_{j\in[N]}(\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},y^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{y}^{j}))\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\|\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},y^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{y}^{j})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{\leq}}\frac{2}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\left\|\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},y^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},y^{j})\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},y^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{y}^{j})\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{\leq}}\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\left\|x^{i}-\bar{x}^{i}\right\|^{2}+\left\|y^{j}-\bar{y}^{j}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(7)}}{{=}}\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\left\|\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\|\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(8)}}{{=}}2L^{2}\left(\left\|\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(9)}}{{=}}2L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}.

In the above, steps (1) and (2) are by definitions; in step (3) we pull the
1/N
outside the square.
Step (4) follows from Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
\|\sum_{j\in[N]}a_{j}\|^{2}\leq N\sum_{j\in[N]}\|a_{j}\|^{2}
).
In step (5) we introduce an intermediate term
\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},y^{j})
and use the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
.
In step (6) we use the property that
\nabla_{x}V
is
L
-Lipschitz, and note that in the first term the
y^{j}
part is common, while in the second term the
\bar{x}^{i}
part is common.
In step (7) we write the previous terms in vector notation.
In step (8) we collect the terms inside the previous summation which are all equal.
In the last step (9) we use the definitions
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and
\bar{\mathbf{z}}=(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})
.

By a similar argument and using the
L
-Lipschitz property of
\nabla_{y}V
, we can show the second term is also bounded by the same quantity:

\displaystyle\left\|b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})\right\|^{2}\leq 2L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}.

Combining the two bounds above, we obtain:

\displaystyle\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|^{2}\,\leq\,2L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}+2L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}\,=\,4L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}

which shows that
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(2L)
-Lipschitz.

(2)

We now show that
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
This is equivalent to showing that the symmetrized Jacobian of
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
satisfies, for all
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})+\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})^{\top}\right)\preceq-\alpha I.

(43)

Indeed, if we have (
43
), then for all
\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, by the mean-value theorem we can write:

\displaystyle\left\langle b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}),\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\int_{0}^{1}\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{t})\,(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\,dt,\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})^{\top}\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{t})\,(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})^{\top}(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{t}))_{\mathrm{sym}}\,(\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}})\,dt

\displaystyle\leq-\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}\,dt

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}

where in the above we have defined
\mathbf{z}_{t}:=(1-t)\mathbf{z}+t\bar{\mathbf{z}}
for
0\leq t\leq 1
, and used the property that
u^{\top}Au=\frac{1}{2}u^{\top}(A+A^{\top})u=u^{\top}(A_{\mathrm{sym}})u
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{D}
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
.

To show (
43
), we compute the Jacobian matrix
\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN\times 2dN}
, which is a block matrix with
2N
blocks on each dimension, indexed by
x^{1},\dots,x^{N},y^{1},\dots,y^{N}
, with the following entries:

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))[x^{i},x^{i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[x^{i}]=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{xx}V(x^{i},y^{k})\qquad

\displaystyle\forall~i\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))[x^{i},x^{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[x^{i}]=0

\displaystyle\forall~i\neq j\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))[x^{i},y^{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[x^{i}]=-\frac{1}{N}\nabla^{2}_{yx}V(x^{i},y^{j})

\displaystyle\forall~i,j\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))[y^{i},y^{i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[y^{i}]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{yy}V(x^{k},y^{i})\qquad

\displaystyle\forall~i\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))[y^{i},y^{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[y^{i}]=0

\displaystyle\forall~i\neq j\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))[y^{i},x^{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[y^{i}]=\frac{1}{N}\nabla^{2}_{xy}V(x^{j},y^{i})

\displaystyle\forall~i,j\in[N].

Now for the symmetrized Jacobian
(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}
, we can compute its block entries:

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[x^{i},x^{i}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{xx}V(x^{i},y^{k})\qquad

\displaystyle\forall~i\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[x^{i},x^{j}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\forall~i\neq j\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[x^{i},y^{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[x^{i},y^{j}]+\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[y^{j},x^{i}]^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{1}{N}\nabla^{2}_{yx}V(x^{i},y^{j})+\frac{1}{N}\nabla^{2}_{xy}V(x^{i},y^{j})^{\top}\right)=0

\displaystyle\forall~i,j\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[y^{i},y^{i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{yy}V(x^{k},y^{i})\qquad

\displaystyle\forall~i\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[y^{i},y^{j}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\forall~i\neq j\in[N],

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[y^{i},x^{j}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[y^{i},x^{j}]+\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})[x^{j},y^{i}]^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{N}\nabla^{2}_{xy}V(x^{j},y^{i})-\frac{1}{N}\nabla^{2}_{yx}V(x^{j},y^{i})^{\top}\right)=0

\displaystyle\forall~i,j\in[N].

Therefore, we see that the symmetrized Jacobian
(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}
is block-diagonal, since all the off-diagonal block entries are
0
.
Moreover, from the assumption that
V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly convex in
x
and
\alpha
-strongly concave in
y
, the block-diagonal entries satisfy, for all
i\in[N]
:

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[x^{i},x^{i}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{xx}V(x^{i},y^{k})\preceq-\alpha I

\displaystyle(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}[y^{i},y^{i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\in[N]}\nabla^{2}_{yy}V(x^{k},y^{i})\preceq-\alpha I.

This shows that
(\nabla b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}))_{\mathrm{sym}}\preceq-\alpha I
, and thus
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, as desired.
∎

D.1.2
Properties of the mean-field vector field

Lemma 13
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then:

1.

The vector field
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
40
) is
L
-Lipschitz, which means for all
\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|\leq L\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\|.

2.

Furthermore,
-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, which means for all
\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\left\langle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}),\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rangle\leq-\alpha\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(x^{1},\dots,x^{N},y^{1},\dots,y^{N})

and
\bar{\mathbf{z}}=(\bar{\mathbf{x}},\bar{\mathbf{y}})=(\bar{x}^{1},\dots,\bar{x}^{N},\bar{y}^{1},\dots,\bar{y}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
be given.

1.

We first show
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
L
-Lipschitz.
By assumption,
(x,y)\mapsto\nabla V(x,y)
is
L
-Lipschitz; in particular, each component
\nabla_{x}V(x,y)
and
\nabla_{y}V(x,y)
is also
L
-Lipschitz.
By definition,

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}})\right\|^{2}+\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{y}})\right\|^{2}.

We can bound the first term as:

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},\bar{Y})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{Y})]\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},\bar{Y})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{Y})\right]\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq L^{2}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|x^{i}-\bar{x}^{i}\right]\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2}

where the first inequality is by Cauchy-Schwarz, and the second inequality is by the
L
-Lipschitz property of
\nabla_{x}V
.
Similarly, using the
L
-Lipschitz property of
\nabla_{y}V
, we can bound the second term as:

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{y}})\right\|^{2}\leq L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\|^{2}.

Combining the two bounds above gives:

\displaystyle\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2}+L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\|^{2}\,=\,L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}

which shows that
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
L
-Lipschitz.

2.

We now show
-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
By assumption, for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
x\mapsto V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly convex, so
x\mapsto\nabla_{x}V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
Similarly, for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
y\mapsto V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly concave, so
y\mapsto-\nabla_{y}V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
By definition,

\displaystyle\left\langle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}),\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}),\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle+\left\langle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{y}}),\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\rangle.

We can bound the first term as:

\displaystyle\left\langle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{x}}),\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[-\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},\bar{Y})]+\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{Y})],x^{i}-\bar{x}^{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\langle\nabla_{x}V(x^{i},\bar{Y})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{x}^{i},\bar{Y}),x^{i}-\bar{x}^{i}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq-\alpha\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|x^{i}-\bar{x}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\|\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2}

where the inequality above follows from the
\alpha
-strong monotonicity of
x\mapsto\nabla_{x}V(x,\bar{Y})
.

Similarly, we can bound the second term as:

\displaystyle\left\langle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{y}}),\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[N]}\left\langle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X},y^{i})]-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}[\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X},\bar{y}^{i})],y^{i}-\bar{y}^{i}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}\left[\left\langle\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X},y^{i})-\nabla_{y}V(\bar{X},\bar{y}^{i}),y^{i}-\bar{y}^{i}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq-\alpha\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}\left[\left\|y^{i}-\bar{y}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-\alpha\left\|\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\|^{2}

where the inequality above follows from the
\alpha
-strong monotonicity of
y\mapsto-\nabla_{y}V(X,\bar{y})
.

Combining the two bounds above gives:

\displaystyle\left\langle\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}}),\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\leq-\alpha\left\|\mathbf{x}-\bar{\mathbf{x}}\right\|^{2}-\alpha\left\|\mathbf{y}-\bar{\mathbf{y}}\right\|^{2}\,=\,-\alpha\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}

which shows that
-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.

∎

D.2
Comparison Between the Vector Fields

D.2.1
Comparison at stationary distribution

Lemma 14
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then the vector fields
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
17
) and
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
40
) satisfy:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

Proof.

Let
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\mathbf{X}},\bar{\mathbf{Y}})=(\bar{X}^{1},\dots,\bar{X}^{N},\bar{Y}^{1},\dots,\bar{Y}^{N})\sim\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{X})^{\otimes N}\otimes(\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{\otimes N}
, so all the random variables
\bar{X}^{i}\sim\bar{\nu}^{X}
and
\bar{Y}^{j}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
are independent, for all
i,j\in[N]
.

By definition, the quantity we wish to bound is:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{X}})-b^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{X}},\bar{\mathbf{Y}})\right\|^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{Y}})-b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{X}},\bar{\mathbf{Y}})\right\|^{2}\right].

We bound each term in the right-hand side separately.

We will use the following formula for variance:
If
U
and
U^{\prime}
are independent random variables with the same distribution
\rho
, then

\mathsf{Var}_{\rho}(U)=\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[\|U-\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[U]\|^{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho\otimes\rho}[\|U-U^{\prime}\|^{2}].

We bound the first term above.
We introduce an independent random variable
\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
.
Then:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{X}})-b^{\mathbf{X}}(\bar{\mathbf{X}},\bar{\mathbf{Y}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{X}(\bar{X}^{i})-b^{X}(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{\mathbf{Y}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y})\right]+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y}^{j})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y}^{j})-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y})\right]\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y}^{j})-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y})\right]\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y}^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{\leq}}\frac{L^{2}}{2N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\bar{Y}^{j}-\bar{Y}^{j}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(7)}}{{=}}\frac{L^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}(\bar{Y})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(8)}}{{=}}L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}(\bar{Y}).

Above, in step (1) we use the definitions of
b^{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}
and split the squared norm across coordinates.
In step (2), we use the definitions of
b^{X}
and
\bar{b}^{X}
.
In step (3), we pull the
1/N
outside the square.
In step (4), we expand the square and note the cross terms are zero since all the random variables are independent;
at this point, we recognize each term in the summation, conditioned on
\bar{X}^{i}
, is the variance of the random variable
\nabla_{x}V(\bar{X}^{i},\bar{Y}^{j})
where
\bar{Y}^{j}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
.
In step (5), we use the variance formula by introducing an independent random variable
\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
.
In step (6), we use the property that
y\mapsto\nabla_{x}V(x,y)
is
L
-Lipschitz, which follows from the smoothness of
V
from Assumption
1
.
In step (7), we use the variance formula for the random variable
\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
.
In the last step (8), we collect the terms in the double summation which are all the same.

By an identical argument, we can also bound the
Y
-component in the quantity above as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{Y}})-b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\bar{\mathbf{X}},\bar{\mathbf{Y}})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}(\bar{X}).

Combining the two calculations above, we obtain the desired bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}(\bar{Y})+L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}(\bar{X})=L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

where the last step follows from the fact that
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}
is a product distribution.
∎

D.2.2
Comparison at arbitrary distribution

Lemma 15
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
For any
\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
, the vector fields
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
17
) and
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
40
) satisfy:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2L^{2}\,W_{2}(\rho^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})=(X^{1},\dots,X^{N},Y^{1},\dots,Y^{N})\sim\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
We first introduce some set up.

Let
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{N}\colon[N]\to[N]
be a collection of permutations such that
\sigma_{j}(i)\neq\sigma_{k}(i)
for all
j\neq k
and
i\in[N]
.
For example, we can take
\sigma_{j}(i)=i+j\pmod{N}
.

We introduce new random variables
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{1},\dots,\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, where each
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{i}=(\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{i},\bar{\mathbf{Y}}^{i})=(\bar{X}^{i,1},\dots,\bar{X}^{i,N},\bar{Y}^{i,1},\dots,\bar{Y}^{i,N})
with
\bar{X}^{i,j},\bar{Y}^{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for each
i,j\in[N]
, with the following structure of joint distribution:

•

For each
i\in[N]
,
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{i}\sim\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
marginally, and
(\mathbf{Z},\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{i})
is jointly distributed as the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, so
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{Z}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{i}\|^{2}]=W_{2}(\rho^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}
.

•

The random variables
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{1},\dots,\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{N}
are pairwise independent conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}
.
In particular, this implies that for all
i,j,k\in[N]
with
j\neq k
:
(1)
\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j}
and
\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{k}(i),k}
are independent when conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}
, since
\sigma_{j}(i)\neq\sigma_{k}(i)
; and similarly,
(2)
\bar{X}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j}
and
\bar{X}^{\sigma_{k}(i),k}
are independent when conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}
.

By definition, the quantity we wish to bound is:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})\right\|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X})-b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{Y})-b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\right\|^{2}\right].

We will bound each term in the right-hand side separately.

We bound the first term above.
Below, we write
\mathbb{E}
to denote the expectation over the collective joint distribution of the random variables
(\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{Z}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{Z}^{N})
introduced above.
We can bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}

\displaystyle\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X})-b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{X}(X^{i})-b^{X}(X^{i},\mathbf{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[-\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y})]+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\in[N]}\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},Y^{j})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},Y^{j})+\mathbb{E}_{\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[-\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y})]\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},Y^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j})\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j})-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y})]\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=:\mathsf{Err}_{1}^{X}+\mathsf{Err}_{2}^{X}.

(44)

In the above, we have used the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
.

We can bound the first error term in (
44
) by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Err}_{1}^{X}

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},Y^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j})\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},Y^{j})-\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y^{j}-\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|Y^{j}-\bar{Y}^{i,j}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Y}-\bar{\mathbf{Y}}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right].

In the above, the first inequality is by Cauchy-Schwarz (
\|\sum_{j\in[N]}a_{j}\|^{2}\leq N\sum_{j\in[N]}\|a_{j}\|^{2}
);
the next inequality is by the smoothness assumption on
V
;
the next step is by rearranging the permutation;
and in the last step we rewrite the expression in terms of the vector form.

We can bound the second error term in (
44
) by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Err}_{2}^{X}

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sum_{j\in[N]}\left(\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j})-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y})]\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j})-\mathbb{E}_{\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y})]\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leq}}\frac{2}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j})-\nabla_{x}V(X^{i},\bar{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\leq}}\frac{2L^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j}-\bar{Y}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{=}}\frac{4L^{2}}{N^{2}}\sum_{i\in[N]}\sum_{j\in[N]}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}(\bar{Y})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{=}}4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}(\bar{Y}).

In the above, step (1) follows by expanding the square and noting the cross terms are zero; this follows from our construction, since for each
i
and for each
j\neq k
, when conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}
(which includes
X^{i}
), the random variables
\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j}
and
\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{k}(i),k}
are independent (recall
\sigma_{j}(i)\neq\sigma_{k}(i)
), and each term in the summation has mean
0
.
The next step (2) follows by introducing an independent random variable
\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to pull the expectation outside the square.
The next step (3) follows from the smoothness assumption on
V
from Assumption
1
.
The next step (4) follows by recognizing each term in the double summation is a variance of
\bar{Y}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
(recall
\bar{Y}^{\sigma_{j}(i),j}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Y}
also and is independent of
\bar{Y}
).
The last step (5) is by collecting the terms in the double summation which are all equal.

Plugging in the two calculations above to (
44
), we can control the first term in the quantity we wish to bound as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X})-b^{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Y}-\bar{\mathbf{Y}}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}(\bar{Y}).

By an identical argument, we can control the second term in the quantity we wish to bound as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{Y})-b^{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{X}-\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}(\bar{X}).

Combining the two bounds above, and recalling that each
(\mathbf{Z},\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{i})
has the optimal
W_{2}
coupling, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Y}-\bar{\mathbf{Y}}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}(\bar{Y})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{X}-\bar{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}(\bar{X})

\displaystyle=\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}^{i}\right\|^{2}\right]+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle=\frac{2L^{2}}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}W_{2}(\rho^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle=2L^{2}W_{2}(\rho^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

which is the desired bound.
∎

D.3
Bounds on the Vector Fields

D.3.1
Bound on the mean-field vector field under mean-field distribution

We bound the magnitude of the mean-field vector field under the stationary mean-field distribution, which is proportional to the Fisher information.

Lemma 16
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then for the mean-field vector field
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
40
), under the tensorized stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
18
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2\tau dLN.

Proof.

Recall by construction, as stated in (
41
), that the mean-field vector field
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is a scaled score function of the tensorized stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, i.e.,

\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})=\tau\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})
.
Note from the definition (
10
), since
V
is
L
-smooth,
\log\bar{\nu}^{X}
and
\log\bar{\nu}^{Y}
are
(L/\tau)
-smooth, and thus
\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is also
(L/\tau)
-smooth, i.e.,

\left\|\nabla^{2}\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\right\|_{\mathsf{op}}\leq L/\tau
for all
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
In particular,

\displaystyle\Delta\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})=\mathsf{Tr}\left(\nabla^{2}\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\right)\leq\frac{L}{\tau}\cdot 2dN.

Using integration by parts, where the boundary term is
0
since
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\to 0
as
\|\mathbf{z}\|\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\left\langle\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}),\,\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\right\rangle\,d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=-\tau^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\left\langle\nabla\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}),\,\nabla\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\right\rangle\,d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=\tau^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\,\Delta\log\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\,d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle\leq 2\tau dLN.

∎

D.3.2
Bound on the finite-particle vector field under mean-field distribution

Lemma 17
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then for the finite-particle vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
17
), and for the tensorized stationary mean-field distribution (
18
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+4\tau dLN.

Proof.

We can bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})+\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+4\tau dLN.

In the above, we have introduced an intermediate term
\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})
, used the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
, and used the results from Lemma
14
and Lemma
16
.
∎

D.3.3
Bound on the finite-particle vector field under arbitrary distribution

Lemma 18
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then for the finite-particle vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
defined in (
17
), and for any distribution
\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 8L^{2}\,W_{2}(\rho^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+8\tau dLN.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{Z}\sim\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}\sim\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
such that
(\mathbf{Z},\bar{\mathbf{Z}})
has the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
We use
\mathbb{E}
to denote the expectation over this joint coupling.
Then we can bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})+b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 8L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}\right\|^{2}\right]+2\left(2L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+4\tau dLN\right)

\displaystyle=8L^{2}\,W_{2}(\rho^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+8\tau dLN.

In the above, we introduce an additional term
b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}})
with the coupling defined above, and use the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
.
In the next step, we use the property that
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(2L)
-Lipschitz from Lemma
12
, and the bound from Lemma
17
.
In the last step, we use the fact that
(\mathbf{Z},\bar{\mathbf{Z}})
has the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
∎

D.4
Time Derivative of KL Divergence Along Fokker-Planck Equations

We have the following formula on the time derivative of KL divergence of two distributions which evolve following their Fokker-Planck equations, which can have different drift terms, but with the same diffusion term.
This is a classical formula that has been used in many previous works, including for analyzing stochastic interpolants
(
Albergo et al., 2023
, Lemma 2.2)
and for showing the propagation of chaos in interacting particle systems
(
Lacker and Le Flem, 2023
, Lemma 3.1)
.

Lemma 19
.

Suppose
(\rho_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\bar{\rho}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
are probability distributions in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{D})
which evolve following the Fokker-Planck equations:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}b_{t})+c\Delta\rho_{t}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot(\bar{\rho}_{t}\bar{b}_{t})+c\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}

for some time-dependent vector fields
b_{t},\bar{b}_{t}\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{D}
, and for some constant
c\geq 0
.
Then:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t})=-c\,\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t})+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}}\left[\left\langle\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}},\,b_{t}-\bar{b}_{t}\right\rangle\right].

Proof.

We can compute by differentiating under the integral sign and using chain rule:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\,dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\frac{1}{\rho_{t}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}\,dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\frac{1}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}}{\partial t}.

(45)

We compute each term above.
For the first term in (
45
),
by the Fokker-Planck equation and using integration by parts, where all the boundary terms vanish:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\left(-\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}b_{t})+c\Delta\rho_{t}\right)\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle b_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx-c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle\nabla\log\rho_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx

where for the second term above we have used the identity that
\Delta\rho_{t}=\nabla\cdot\nabla\rho_{t}=\nabla\cdot(\rho_{t}\nabla\log\rho_{t})
.

For the second term in (
45
), we can show it is equal to
0
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\frac{1}{\rho_{t}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}\,dx\,=\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\,dx\,=\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(1)\,=\,0.

For the third term in (
45
),
by the Fokker-Planck equation and using integration by parts:

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\frac{1}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\left(-\nabla\cdot(\bar{\rho}_{t}\bar{b}_{t})+c\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}\right)\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\,dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\bar{\rho}_{t}\left\langle\bar{b}_{t},\nabla\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx+c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\bar{\rho}_{t}\left\langle\nabla\log\bar{\rho}_{t},\nabla\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle\bar{b}_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx+c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle\nabla\log\bar{\rho}_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx

where in the above we have used the identity that
\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}=\nabla\cdot(\bar{\rho}_{t}\nabla\log\bar{\rho}_{t})
,
and
\bar{\rho}_{t}\nabla\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}=\rho_{t}\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}
.

Combining the three terms above in (
45
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle b_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx-c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle\nabla\log\rho_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle\bar{b}_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx+c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle\nabla\log\bar{\rho}_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\langle b_{t}-\bar{b}_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\,dx-c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\rho_{t}\left\|\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}}\left[\left\langle b_{t}-\bar{b}_{t},\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}}\right\rangle\right]-c\,\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t})

as desired.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs for the Finite-Particle Dynamics

E.1
Proof of Theorem
4
(Biased Convergence of the Finite-Particle Dynamics)

Proof of Theorem
4
.

(1) Biased
W_{2}
convergence bound:
We use the synchronous coupling technique.
Concretely, we consider
(\mathbf{Z}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, where
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the finite-particle dynamics (
16
):

\displaystyle d\mathbf{Z}_{t}=b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

and where
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the stationary tensorized mean-field dynamics (
16
):

\displaystyle d\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}=\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

where we start from the stationary distribution
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, so
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
for all
t\geq 0
.
Suppose we run the two stochastic processes above using the same standard Brownian motion
(W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
Furthermore, suppose we start the two processes above from
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0})
which has a joint distribution which is the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.

With the set up above, the difference
\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}
evolves via:

\displaystyle d(\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})=\left(b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right)dt

where the Brownian motion terms cancel because they are equal in the two processes.
Then we get:

\displaystyle d\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}=2\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle dt.

Taking expectation over the joint distribution, we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right],

where in the last step we have introduced an intermediate term
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]
.

We can bound the two terms above separately as follows.
For the first term, recall from Lemma
12
that
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, so we can bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]\leq-\alpha\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right].

For the second term, we use the inequality
\langle a,b\rangle\leq\frac{\alpha}{4}\|a\|^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\|b\|^{2}
, and apply the bound from Lemma
14
to get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{4}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{4}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{L^{2}}{\alpha}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

Plugging in these two bounds to the computation above, we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{3\alpha}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

This is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

Integrating from
0
to
t
and rearranging yields:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{1-e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}}{\frac{3}{2}\alpha}\right)\,\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})
