\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]+\,\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\,\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

where in the second inequality above we drop the
-e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}
term and further bound
\frac{4}{3}\leq 2
,
and in the last step we use the fact that
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0})
has the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
Finally, since
W_{2}
distance is the infimum over all coupling, from the above inequality we conclude:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\,\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

(46)

as desired.

(2) Biased convergence in KL divergence:

We consider
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
which evolves following the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, so
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}.

We also consider
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
which evolves following the stationary tensorized mean-field dynamics (
42
) from
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, so
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
satisfies the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\tau\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}.

(Note since
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, both sides of the Fokker-Planck equation above are in fact equal to
0
.)

Then using the formula from Lemma
19
, we can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}-\tau\,\mathsf{FI}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\langle\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leq}}-\frac{\tau}{2}\,\mathsf{FI}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{1}{2\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\leq}}-\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{1}{2\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{\leq}}-\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{\leq}}-\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau}\left(\frac{L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}+1\right)\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{\leq}}-\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{2}\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

In the above, in step (1) we use the time derivative formula for KL divergence from Lemma
19
.
In step (2), we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality (
\langle a,b\rangle\leq\frac{\tau}{2}\|a\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\tau}\|b\|^{2}
) to the second term.
In step (3), we use the fact that
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-strongly log-concave by Lemma
2
, so it also satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-LSI, which allows us to bound the relative Fisher information by KL divergence.
In step (4), we use the bound from Lemma
15
.
In step (5), we use the
W_{2}
biased convergence bound (
46
) that we derived earlier, in the first part of this Theorem.
In step (6), we bound
1\leq L^{2}/\alpha^{2}
since
\alpha\leq L
.

Let us now substitute
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
Then we can write the differential inequality above as:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{\alpha t}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+e^{\alpha t}\,\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{2}\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

Integrating from
0
to
t
and rearranging yields:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha t}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+e^{-\alpha t}\left(\frac{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha t}}{\frac{1}{2}\alpha}\right)\frac{L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\left(\frac{1-e^{-\alpha t}}{\alpha}\right)\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{2}\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha t}\left(\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha t}\left(\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+\frac{4L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

as desired.
∎

E.2
Proof of Corollary
5
(Average Particle Along the Finite-Particle Dynamics)

Proof of Corollary
5
.

For
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=(\mathbf{X}_{t},\mathbf{Y}_{t})=(X_{t}^{1},\dots,X_{t}^{N},Y_{t}^{1},\dots,Y_{t}^{N})\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, let
\rho_{t}^{Z,i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
be the marginal distribution of the component
Z_{t}^{i}=(X_{t}^{i},Y_{t}^{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, for
i\in[N]
.
We rearrange the coordinates to write
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=(Z_{t}^{1},\dots,Z_{t}^{N})
for convenience, and still denote its distribution by
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
We introduce an independent product of the marginal distributions:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}:=\bigotimes_{i\in[N]}\rho_{t}^{Z,i}.

Since
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{X})^{\otimes N}\otimes(\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{\otimes N}
is an independent product, after rearranging the coordinates as above, we can write it as
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{\otimes N}
where
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}
.

Then we can bound the KL divergence by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\hat{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\log\frac{\hat{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{(\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{\otimes N}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\hat{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\right]+\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z,i}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{t}^{Z,i}}{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\hat{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{Z,i}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{Z,i}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)

where the inequality follows by dropping the first term
\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\hat{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)\geq 0
, which is the multivariate mutual information of
\mathbf{Z}_{t}
.
Furthermore, since KL divergence is jointly convex in both arguments, we can further bound the above by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle\geq N\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{Z,i}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)\,\geq\,N\cdot\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)

using the definition
\rho_{t}^{Z,\mathrm{avg}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\rho_{t}^{Z,i}
.

Combining this with the bound for
\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})
from Theorem
4
gives the desired result.
∎

Appendix F
Proofs for the Finite-Particle Algorithm

F.1
Preliminary Results

F.1.1
Bound in one step of the finite-particle algorithm

We present the following bound which will be useful in our subsequent analysis.

Lemma 20
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Let
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
for any
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
For
t\geq 0
, define
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{Z}_{0}+tb^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})+\sqrt{2\tau t}\,\bm{\zeta}

where
\bm{\zeta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is an independent Gaussian random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, and
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is the vector field defined in (
17
).
If
0\leq t\leq 1/(4L)
, then:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 128t^{2}L^{4}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+64t^{2}L^{4}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\tau tdL^{2}N.

Proof.

We can bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leq}}4L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\mathbf{Z}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}4L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|tb^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})+\sqrt{2\tau t}\,\bm{\zeta}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{=}}4t^{2}L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\right\|^{2}\right]+8\tau tL^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\bm{\zeta}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{=}}4t^{2}L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\right\|^{2}\right]+16\tau tdL^{2}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{\leq}}8t^{2}L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+8t^{2}L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+16\tau tdL^{2}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+8t^{2}L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+16\tau tdL^{2}N.

In the above, in step (1) we have used the property that
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(2L)
-Lipschitz from Lemma
12
.
In step (2), we plug in the definition of
\mathbf{Z}_{t}
.
In step (3), we expand the square, and note the cross term vanishes since
\bm{\zeta}
is independent of
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
and
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\zeta}]=0
.
In step (4), we use the property that
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\zeta}\|^{2}]=2dN
since
\bm{\zeta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is a standard Gaussian in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
In step (5), we introduce the term
b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})
again, and use the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
.
In step (6), we use the assumption that
t\leq 1/(4L)
, so
8t^{2}L^{2}\leq 1/2
.

Rearranging the inequality above, we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(7)}}{{\leq}}16t^{2}L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+32\tau tdL^{2}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(8)}}{{\leq}}16t^{2}L^{2}\left(8L^{2}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+8\tau dLN\right)+32\tau tdL^{2}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(9)}}{{=}}128t^{2}L^{4}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+64t^{2}L^{4}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+32\tau tdL^{2}N(4tL+1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(10)}}{{\leq}}128t^{2}L^{4}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+64t^{2}L^{4}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\tau tdL^{2}N.

In the above, in step (7) we rearrange the inequality from step (6).
In step (8), we use the bound from Lemma
18
for the distribution
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
In step (9), we collect the terms from the previous line.
In step (10), we again use the assumption that
t\leq 1/(4L)
.
This gives us the desired bound.
∎

F.1.2
Fokker-Planck equation for one-step interpolation of finite-particle algorithm

We show the following continuous-time interpolation of one step of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
).
This is similar to the interpolation technique that has been used, for example, to analyze the mixing time of the Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm for sampling
(
Vempala and Wibisono, 2019
)
.

Lemma 21
.

Let
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
for any
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
For
t\geq 0
, define
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{Z}_{0}+tb^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})+\sqrt{2t\tau}\,\bm{\zeta}

(47)

where
\bm{\zeta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is an independent standard Gaussian random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, and
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is the vector field defined in (
17
).
Then the density
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

where we define the vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
by, for all
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\displaystyle b^{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\mid\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{z}\right]

where
\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z})
is the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
given
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{z}
from the model (
47
).

Proof.

Let
\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
be the joint distribution of
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\mathbf{Z}_{t})
following the model (
47
), which we can write in terms of the marginal and conditional distributions as, for all
\mathbf{z}_{0},\mathbf{z}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\displaystyle\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0},\mathbf{z}_{t})=\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{t}\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})=\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{t})\,\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0}\mid\mathbf{z}_{t}).

Notice that
\mathbf{Z}_{t}
as defined in (
47
) is the solution to the following stochastic process:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{Z}_{t}=b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

(48)

where
(W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})_{t\geq 0}
is the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
which is independent of
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
.
We derive the Fokker-Planck equation for
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
as follows.
First, when we condition on a fixed
\mathbf{Z}_{0}=\mathbf{z}_{0}
, the drift in the process (
48
) is a constant, so the conditional density
\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})
of
\mathbf{Z}_{t}
conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}_{0}=\mathbf{z}_{0}
is given by the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})

where note that the divergence
\nabla\cdot
and Laplacian
\Delta
operate on the
\mathbf{z}
variable, not
\mathbf{z}_{0}
.

Then we can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})\,d\mathbf{z}_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,\frac{\partial\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})}{\partial t}\,d\mathbf{z}_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,\left(-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})\right)\,d\mathbf{z}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,d\mathbf{z}_{0}\right)+\tau\Delta\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,\rho_{t\mid 0}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z}_{0})\,d\mathbf{z}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0}\mid\cdot)\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,d\mathbf{z}_{0}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\mid\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\cdot\right]\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},

as desired.
∎

F.2
One-Step Recurrence for the Biased Convergence of the Finite-Particle Algorithm

F.2.1
One-step recurrence in
W_{2}
distance

Lemma 22
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Let
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
for any
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
, and let
\mathbf{z}_{k+1}\sim\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
be one step of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
0<\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
.
Then:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}\,W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{8\eta L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right).

Proof.

We consider a continuous-time interpolation of one step of the algorithm (
20
) as follows.
Let
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
where we define
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.
We define
(\mathbf{Z}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq\eta}
where
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{Z}_{t}=b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

(49)

where
(W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})_{t\geq 0}
is the standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
which is independent of
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
.
Then we notice that the distribution of
\mathbf{Z}_{\eta}\sim\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}
of the process (
49
) is equal to the distribution of the next iterate
\mathbf{x}_{k+1}\sim\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
along the algorithm (
20
), i.e.,
\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
This is because the solution to the process (
49
) at time
t=\eta
is:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Z}_{0}+tb^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})+\sqrt{2\tau}\,W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\,\mathbf{Z}_{0}+tb^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})+\sqrt{2\tau t}\,\bm{\zeta}

where
\bm{\zeta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is an independent Gaussian random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, and
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}
means equality in distribution.
This is the same update of the algorithm (
20
),
so indeed
\mathbf{Z}_{\eta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathbf{z}_{k+1}
, so
\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.

We also consider
(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq\eta}
where
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the stationary tensorized mean-field dynamics (
16
):

\displaystyle d\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}=\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

(50)

starting from the stationary distribution
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, so
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
for all
t\geq 0
.
Suppose we run the stochastic processes (
49
) and (
50
) using the same standard Brownian motion
(W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})_{t\geq 0}
, which is independent of
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
and
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}
.
Furthermore, suppose we start the processes (
49
) and (
50
) from
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0})
which has a joint distribution which is the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.

From the set up above, we have that the difference
\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}
satisfies:

\displaystyle d\left(\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right)=\left(b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right)\,dt

where the Brownian motion terms cancel because they are equal in the two processes.
Therefore:

\displaystyle d\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}=2\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle dt.

Taking expectation over the joint distribution of all the variables, we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})+b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right].

(51)

By Lemma
13
,
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, so we can bound the first term above by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]\leq-\alpha\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right].

We can bound the second term above by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})+b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\alpha}{8}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2}{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})+b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\alpha}{8}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{4}{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{4}{\alpha}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\alpha}{8}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{4}{\alpha}\left(128t^{2}L^{4}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+64t^{2}L^{4}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\tau tdL^{2}N\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{4}{\alpha}\,L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{\leq}}\left(\frac{\alpha}{8}+\frac{512t^{2}L^{4}}{\alpha}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{4L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(64t^{2}L^{2}+1\right)\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+\frac{256\tau tdL^{2}N}{\alpha}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\alpha}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{8L^{2}}{\alpha}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+\frac{256\tau\eta dL^{2}N}{\alpha}.

In the above, in step (1) we use the inequality
\langle a,b\rangle\leq\frac{\alpha}{8}\|a\|^{2}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\|b\|^{2}
.
In step (2), we use the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
to the second term.
In step (3), we use the bound from Lemma
20
to the second term (note
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{64L}\leq\frac{1}{4L}
so the assumption in Lemma
20
is satisfied), and we use the bound from Lemma
14
to the third term.
In step (4), we use the bound
W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]
which follows from the definition of the
W_{2}
distance.
In step (5), we use the assumption
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
, so in the first term,
\frac{512t^{2}L^{4}}{\alpha}\leq\frac{\alpha}{8}
; we also use the assumption
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{64L}
, so in the second term,
64t^{2}L^{2}\leq\frac{1}{64}\leq 1
; and we use
t\leq\eta
in the third term.

Plugging in the two bounds above to our earlier calculation (
51
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{3\alpha}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{8L^{2}}{\alpha}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+\frac{512\tau\eta dL^{2}N}{\alpha}.

We can write this differential inequality equivalently as:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\left(\frac{8L^{2}}{\alpha}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+\frac{512\tau\eta dL^{2}N}{\alpha}\right).

Integrating from
t=0
to
t=\eta
and rearranging yields:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{\eta}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{\eta}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]+\left(\frac{1-e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}}{\frac{3}{2}\alpha}\right)\left(\frac{8L^{2}}{\alpha}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+\frac{512\tau\eta dL^{2}N}{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\left(\frac{1-e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}}{\frac{3}{2}\alpha}\right)\frac{8L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{8\eta L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right).

In the second step above, we have used the assumption that
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0})
has the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
.
In the last step, we use the inequality
1-e^{-c}\leq c
, which holds for all
c=\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta\geq 0
.
Using the definition of the
W_{2}
distance as the infimum over all coupling, from the above, we conclude:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{8\eta L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right).

Substituting back
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
gives us the desired bound.
∎

F.2.2
One-step recurrence in KL divergence

Lemma 23
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Let
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
for any
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
, and let
\mathbf{z}_{k+1}\sim\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
be one step of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
0<\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
.
Then:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta}\left(\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{3\eta L^{2}}{\tau}W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+88\eta^{2}dL^{2}N+\frac{6\eta L^{2}}{\tau}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

Proof.

We consider a continuous-time interpolation of one step of the algorithm (
20
), as in the proof of Lemma
22
, but now we work with the Fokker-Planck equation, rather than the stochastic process.
Let
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
where we define
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.
For
0\leq t\leq\eta
, we define
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{Z}_{0}+tb^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})+\sqrt{2t\tau}\,\bm{\zeta}

(52)

where
\bm{\zeta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is an independent standard Gaussian random variable in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
By the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
22
, the distribution of
\mathbf{Z}_{\eta}\sim\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is equal to the distribution of the next iterate
\mathbf{z}_{k+1}\sim\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
along the algorithm (
20
).
Let
\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
be the joint distribution of
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\mathbf{Z}_{t})
following the model (
52
), and let
\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{z})
be the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{Z}_{0}
given
\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{z}
.
Define the vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
by, for all
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

\displaystyle b^{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\mid\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{z}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0}\mid\mathbf{z})\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{0})\,d\mathbf{z}_{0}.

Then by Lemma
21
, we know that the density
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right)+\tau\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}.

We also define
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\sim\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
which evolves following the stationary tensorized mean-field dynamics (
42
) from
\bar{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\sim\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, so
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
satisfies the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\tau\Delta\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}.

(Note since
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, both sides of the Fokker-Planck equation above are in fact equal to
0
.)

Then using the formula from Lemma
19
, we can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(1)}}{{=}}-\tau\,\mathsf{FI}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\langle\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}},\,b_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(2)}}{{=}}-\tau\,\mathsf{FI}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\langle\nabla\log\frac{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t}),\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(3)}}{{\leq}}-\frac{\tau}{2}\,\mathsf{FI}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{1}{2\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(4)}}{{\leq}}-\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{1}{2\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right].

(53)

In the above, in step (1) we use the time derivative formula for KL divergence from Lemma
19
.
In step (2), we plug in the definition
b^{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}(\mathbf{z})=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})\mid\mathbf{Z}_{t}=\mathbf{z}\right]
, and use the tower property to write the iterated expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0\mid t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
as a joint expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}
over
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\mathbf{Z}_{t})
.
In step (3), we use the inequality
\langle a,b\rangle\leq\frac{\tau}{2}\|a\|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\tau}\|b\|^{2}
to the second term.
In step (4), we use the fact that
\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-strongly log-concave by Lemma
2
, so it also satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-LSI, which allows us to bound the relative Fisher information by KL divergence.

We can bound the second term above by:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\tau}\,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(5)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{0})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{\tau}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-\bar{b}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(6)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{\tau}\left(128t^{2}L^{4}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+64t^{2}L^{4}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\tau tdL^{2}N\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\tau}\left(2L^{2}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+4L^{2}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(7)}}{{\leq}}\frac{3L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{5L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64tdL^{2}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(8)}}{{\leq}}\frac{3L^{2}}{\tau}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{8tL^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau tdN\right)\right)+\frac{5L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64tdL^{2}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(9)}}{{\leq}}e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{3L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+\frac{24\eta L^{4}}{\alpha\tau}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right)+\frac{5L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\eta dL^{2}N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(10)}}{{\leq}}e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{3L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+88\,\eta dL^{2}N+\frac{6L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

In step (5), we introduce an intermediate term
b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})
and use the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
to the second term.
In step (6), we use the bound from Lemma
20
in the second term (note
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{64L}\leq\frac{1}{4L}
so the assumption in Lemma
20
is satisfied), and the bound from Lemma
15
in the third term.
In step (7), we use the assumption
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{64L}
, so
128t^{2}L^{4}\leq L^{2}
and
64t^{2}L^{4}\leq L^{2}
.
In step (8), we apply the
W_{2}
bound from Lemma
22
, applied to
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
when considered as one step of the discrete-time algorithm (
20
) from
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
with step size
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
.
In step (9), we use the bound
t\leq\eta
in the second and fourth terms.
In step (10), we use the bound
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
, so
\frac{24\eta L^{4}}{\alpha\tau}\cdot 64\tau\eta dN\leq 24\eta dL^{2}N
, and we also bound
\frac{24\eta L^{4}}{\alpha\tau}\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\tau}
.

Plugging in the bound above to the calculation in (
53
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{3L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+88\,\eta dL^{2}N+\frac{6L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z}).

We can write this differential inequality equivalently as:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{\alpha t}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha t}\,\frac{3L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}+e^{\alpha t}\left(88\,\eta dL^{2}N+\frac{6L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\right).

Integrating from
t=0
to
t=\eta
and rearranging yields:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+e^{-\alpha\eta}\left(\frac{1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\eta}}{\frac{1}{2}\alpha}\right)\frac{3L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{1-e^{-\alpha\eta}}{\alpha}\right)\left(88\,\eta dL^{2}N+\frac{6L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta}\left(\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\frac{3\eta L^{2}}{\tau}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)+88\,\eta^{2}dL^{2}N+\frac{6\eta L^{2}}{\tau}\,\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

where in the second step above we use the inequality
1-e^{-c}\leq c
for
c=\frac{1}{2}\alpha\eta
in the second term, and for
c=\alpha\eta
in the third term.
Substituting
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
,
\rho_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
, and
\bar{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\rho}_{\eta}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
gives the desired bound.
∎

F.3
Proof of Theorem
6
(Biased Convergence of Finite-Particle Algorithm to Stationary Mean-Field Distribution)

Proof of Theorem
6
.

(1) Biased
W_{2}
convergence:

For simplicity, let
D_{k}:=W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}
.
Recall from Lemma
22
we have the recurrence:

\displaystyle D_{k+1}\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}\,D_{k}+C

where
C:=\frac{8\eta L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right).

Iterating this recurrence gives us:

\displaystyle D_{k}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta k}\,D_{0}+C\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta i}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta k}\,D_{0}+\frac{C}{1-e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta k}\,D_{0}+\frac{C}{\alpha\eta}.

In the second step above, we use the bound
\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta i}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta i}=1/(1-e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta})
.
In the last step, we use the inequality
1-e^{-c}\geq\frac{2}{3}c
for
0\leq c=\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta\leq\frac{3}{4}
, which holds since
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{64\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{8\alpha}
.
Substituting the definition of
D_{k}
and
C
gives the
W_{2}
convergence bound.

(2)Biased convergence in KL divergence:

Let
H_{k}:=\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)
, and
D_{k}=W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}
as before.
Recall from Lemma
23
we have the recurrence:

\displaystyle H_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta}\left(H_{k}+\frac{3\eta L^{2}}{\tau}D_{k}\right)+C^{\prime}

where
C^{\prime}:=88\eta^{2}dL^{2}N+\frac{6\eta L^{2}}{\tau}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})
.
Iterating this recurrence gives us:

\displaystyle H_{k}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}H_{0}+\frac{3\eta L^{2}}{\tau}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\alpha\eta(k-i)}D_{i}+C^{\prime}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\alpha\eta i}.

(54)

By using the
W_{2}
convergence bound we proved above, we can write:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\alpha\eta(k-i)}D_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\alpha\eta(k-i)}\left(e^{-\frac{3}{2}\alpha\eta i}\,D_{0}+\frac{C}{\alpha\eta}\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-\alpha\eta k}D_{0}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\eta i}+\frac{C}{\alpha\eta}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\alpha\eta(k-i)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-\alpha\eta k}D_{0}}{(1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\eta})}+\frac{C}{\alpha\eta}\frac{e^{-\alpha\eta}}{(1-e^{-\alpha\eta})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3e^{-\alpha\eta k}D_{0}}{\alpha\eta}+\frac{3C}{2\alpha^{2}\eta^{2}}

where in the last step we again use the bound
1-e^{-c}\geq\frac{2}{3}c
for
c=\frac{1}{2}\alpha\eta
and
c=\alpha\eta
, and we also bound
e^{-\alpha\eta}\leq 1
.
Plugging in the value
C=\frac{8\eta L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right)
, the middle term in (
54
) can be bounded by:

\displaystyle\frac{3\eta L^{2}}{\tau}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\alpha\eta(k-i)}D_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3\eta L^{2}}{\tau}\left(\frac{3e^{-\alpha\eta k}D_{0}}{\alpha\eta}+\frac{3}{2\alpha^{2}\eta^{2}}\left(\frac{8\eta L^{2}}{\alpha}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\frac{9L^{2}D_{0}}{\alpha\tau}+\frac{36L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right)

For the last term in (
54
), we can bound:

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}e^{-\alpha\eta i}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{1-e^{-\alpha\eta}}\,\leq\,\frac{3C^{\prime}}{2\alpha\eta}\,=\,\frac{132\eta dL^{2}N}{\alpha}+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

where again we use the bound
1-e^{-c}\geq\frac{2}{3}c
.
Plugging in these two bounds to (
54
), we obtain:

\displaystyle H_{k}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}H_{0}+e^{-\alpha\eta k}\frac{9L^{2}D_{0}}{\alpha\tau}+\frac{36L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+64\,\tau\eta dN\right)+\frac{132\eta dL^{2}N}{\alpha}+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})

\displaystyle=e^{-\alpha\eta k}\left(H_{0}+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}D_{0}\right)+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\left(1+\frac{4L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+\frac{6\eta dL^{2}N}{\alpha}\left(22+384\frac{L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}\left(H_{0}+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}D_{0}\right)+\frac{45L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau}\left(\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})+55\,\eta\tau dN\right)

where in the last step we use the bound
1\leq\frac{L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}
to simplify the second and third terms, and bound
6\times(22+384)=2436\leq 2475=45\times 55
.
∎

F.4
Proof of Corollary
7
(Average Particle Along the Finite-Particle Algorithm)

Proof of Corollary
7
.

The proof below follows identically as in the proof of Corollary
5
.

For
\mathbf{z}_{k}=(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k})=(x_{k}^{1},\dots,x_{k}^{N},y_{k}^{1},\dots,y_{k}^{N})\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, let
\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
be the marginal distribution of the component
z_{k}^{i}=(x_{k}^{i},y_{k}^{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, for
i\in[N]
.
We rearrange the coordinates to write
\mathbf{z}_{k}=(z_{k}^{1},\dots,z_{k}^{N})
for convenience, and still denote its distribution by
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.
We introduce an independent product of the marginal distributions:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}:=\bigotimes_{i\in[N]}\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}.

Since
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{X})^{\otimes N}\otimes(\bar{\nu}^{Y})^{\otimes N}
is an independent product, after rearranging the coordinates as above, we can write it as
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{\otimes N}
where
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}
.

Then we can bound the KL divergence by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}{\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}{\hat{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\log\frac{\hat{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}{(\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{\otimes N}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}{\hat{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\right]+\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}}\left[\log\frac{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}}{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\hat{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)+\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)

where the inequality follows by dropping the first term
\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\hat{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)\geq 0
, which is the multivariate mutual information of
\mathbf{z}_{k}^{\eta}
.
Furthermore, since KL divergence is jointly convex in both arguments, we can further bound the above by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle\geq N\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)\,\geq\,N\cdot\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}\right)

using the definition
\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in[N]}\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,i}
.

Combining this with the bound for
\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})
from Theorem
6
gives the desired result.
∎

F.5
Proofs for Iteration Complexity of the Finite-Particle Algorithm

F.5.1
Preliminary results

Recall
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}
is the stationary mean-field distribution, which is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC and
(L/\tau)
-smooth by Lemma
2
, under Assumption
1
.
Recall by Theorem
35
(in Section
H
) that there exists a unique pair of equilibrium points
(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
that satisfies
\nabla V(x^{*},y^{*})=0
.
We first show the following.

Lemma 24
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then for
\bar{Z}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Z}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
:

\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\leq\frac{2\tau d}{\alpha}.

Proof.

Recall that since
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, it also satisfies the
(\alpha/\tau)
-Poincaré inequality
(
Villani, 2009
)
, which means for all smooth functions
\phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}
:

\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\phi(\bar{Z}))\leq\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\nabla\phi(\bar{Z})\right\|^{2}\right].

For each unit vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
\|u\|=1
, by applying the Poincaré inequality to the function
\phi(z)=\langle z,u\rangle
, we get:

\displaystyle u^{\top}\mathsf{Cov}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})u=\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\langle\bar{Z},u\rangle)\leq\frac{\tau}{\alpha}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\left[\left\|u\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{\tau}{\alpha}.

This shows that the covariance matrix
\mathsf{Cov}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}
satisfies:

\mathsf{Cov}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\preceq\frac{\tau}{\alpha}I.

Taking trace gives us the desired result:

\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})=\mathsf{Tr}\left(\mathsf{Cov}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\right)\leq\frac{2\tau d}{\alpha}.

∎

Lemma 25
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
For
\bar{Z}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Z}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, we have:

\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{8\tau dL^{2}}{\alpha^{3}}.

Proof.

Define the vector field
b^{Z}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by, for all
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
:

\displaystyle b^{Z}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\\
\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\end{pmatrix}.

Observe that
b^{Z}
is the case
N=1
of the vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
that we defined in (
17
) (in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}=\mathbb{R}^{2d}
).
Note by definition,
b^{Z}(z^{*})=0
, since both
\nabla_{x}V(z^{*})=\nabla_{y}V(z^{*})=0
.
Recall by Lemma
12
that
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, so for all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
:

\displaystyle\alpha\|z-z^{*}\|^{2}\leq\langle-b^{Z}(z)+b^{Z}(z^{*}),z-z^{*}\rangle\leq\|b^{Z}(z)-b^{Z}(z^{*})\|\cdot\|z-z^{*}\|

where the second inequality is by Cauchy-Schwarz.
Then we conclude that:

\alpha\|z-z^{*}\|\leq\|b^{Z}(z)-b^{Z}(z^{*})\|=\|b^{Z}(z)\|.

Therefore, for
\bar{Z}\sim\bar{\nu}^{Z}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\left[\left\|b^{Z}(\bar{Z})\right\|^{2}\right]\,\leq\,\frac{2L^{2}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{4\tau dL}{\alpha^{2}}\,\leq\,\frac{4\tau dL^{2}}{\alpha^{3}}+\frac{4\tau dL}{\alpha^{2}}\,\leq\,\frac{8\tau dL^{2}}{\alpha^{3}}

where the second inequality is by Lemma
17
for the case
N=1
,
the third inequality is by the bound on the variance of
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
from Lemma
24
,
and the last inequality is by the bound
1\leq\frac{L}{\alpha}
.
∎

F.5.2
Bound on the initial relative Fisher information

We have the following bounds on the distances from the stationary mean-field distribution from a Gaussian starting distribution.

Lemma 26
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Let
\rho^{X}=\mathcal{N}(m^{X},\frac{\tau}{L}I)
and
\rho^{Y}=\mathcal{N}(m^{Y},\frac{\tau}{L}I)
for arbitrary
m^{X},m^{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and let
\rho^{Z}=\rho^{X}\otimes\rho^{Y}=\mathcal{N}(m^{Z},\frac{\tau}{L}I)
where
m^{Z}=(m^{X},m^{Y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Then:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{22dL^{4}}{\tau\alpha^{3}}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{11dL^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}+\frac{L^{2}}{\tau\alpha}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho^{Z},\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{22\tau dL^{4}}{\alpha^{5}}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}.

Proof.

Since
\rho^{Z}=\rho^{X}\otimes\rho^{Y}
and
\bar{\nu}^{Z}=\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}
are product distributions, we have

\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})=\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})+\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y}).

We will bound each term separately.

Define
g^{X}=-\log\bar{\nu}^{X}
, so
\nabla g^{X}(x)=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(x,\bar{Y})]

and
\Delta g^{X}(x)=\tau^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\Delta_{x}V(x,\bar{Y})]
.
Since we assume
V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly convex in
x
, we have
\Delta g^{X}(x)\geq\alpha d/\tau\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Note also that for
\rho^{X}=\mathcal{N}(m^{X},\frac{\tau}{L}I)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\rho^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|X-m^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{Ld}{\tau}.

Then by expanding the square and using integration by parts, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\rho^{X}+\nabla g^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\rho^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]-2\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\Delta g^{X}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla g^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Ld}{\tau}+\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla g^{X}\right\|^{2}\right].

For the second term above, we can bound:

\displaystyle\tau^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|\nabla g^{X}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(X,\bar{Y})]\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(X,\bar{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(X,\bar{Y})-\nabla_{x}V(x^{*},\bar{Y})\right\|^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{x}V(x^{*},\bar{Y})-\nabla_{x}V(x^{*},y^{*})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2L^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}\left[\left\|X-x^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+2L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\bar{Y}-y^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2L^{2}\left(\frac{\tau d}{L}+\|m^{X}-x^{*}\|^{2}\right)+2L^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\bar{Y}-y^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

In the above, the first inequality follows by Cauchy-Schwarz.
In the second inequality, we introduce the additional term
\nabla_{x}V(x^{*},\bar{Y})
and use the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
, and also introduce
\nabla_{x}V(x^{*},y^{*})=0
.
In the third inequality, we use the property that
V
is
L
-smooth, so
\nabla_{x}V
is
L
-Lipschitz.
The next step follows from the bias-variance decomposition.
Combining the above calculations, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{X}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{X})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3Ld}{\tau}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\|m^{X}-x^{*}\|^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\bar{Y}-y^{*}\right\|^{2}\right].

By an identical argument, we can also bound:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{Y}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Y})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3Ld}{\tau}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\|m^{Y}-y^{*}\|^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}\left[\left\|\bar{X}-x^{*}\right\|^{2}\right].

Combining the two bounds above gives:

\displaystyle\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6Ld}{\tau}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\left(\|m^{X}-x^{*}\|^{2}+\|m^{Y}-y^{*}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}}\left[\left\|\bar{X}-x^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Y}}\left[\left\|\bar{Y}-y^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{6Ld}{\tau}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}\left[\left\|\bar{Z}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6Ld}{\tau}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}+\frac{16dL^{4}}{\tau\alpha^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{22dL^{4}}{\tau\alpha^{3}}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\tau^{2}}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}

where the second inequality follows from Lemma
25
,
and in the last inequality we use the bound
1\leq\frac{L}{\alpha}
to simplify the result.

Since
\bar{\nu}^{Z}
is
(\alpha/\tau)
-SLC, it also satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-LSI and
(\alpha/\tau)
-TI, so we have:

\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\leq\frac{\tau}{2\alpha}\mathsf{FI}(\rho^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})

and

W_{2}(\rho^{Z},\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}\leq\frac{2\tau}{\alpha}\mathsf{KL}(\rho^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z}).

Thus, the bounds for KL divergence and
W_{2}
distance follow from the bound for relative Fisher information above.
∎

F.5.3
Proof of Corollary
8
(Iteration Complexity of the Finite-Particle Algorithm)

Proof of Corollary
8
.

Fix any regularization parameter
\tau>0
, and any small error threshold
\varepsilon>0
.
We want to run the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with a sufficiently small step size
\eta
and a sufficiently large number of particles
N
, for a sufficiently large number of iterations
k
, such that the upper bound on the KL divergence for the average particle in Corollary
7
is less than
\varepsilon
.

We choose the parameters to make each term in the bound from Corollary
7
less than
\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon
.
To do so, we can choose the step size to be:

\displaystyle\eta=\frac{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{3}}{7500\,dL^{4}}\qquad~\Rightarrow~\qquad 2475\frac{\eta dL^{4}}{\alpha^{3}}\,\leq\,2500\frac{\eta dL^{4}}{\alpha^{3}}=\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

We assume
\varepsilon
is small enough so that the choice of
\eta
above satisfies the assumption
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{64L^{2}}
in Corollary
7
;
this is ensured if
\varepsilon\leq\frac{7500}{64}\frac{dL^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}
.

Recall the bound
\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\leq\frac{2\tau d}{\alpha}
from Lemma
24
.
We choose the number of particles to be:

\displaystyle N\geq\frac{270\,dL^{4}}{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{4}}\qquad~\Rightarrow~\qquad\frac{45L^{4}}{\alpha^{3}\tau N}\mathsf{Var}_{\bar{\nu}^{Z}}(\bar{Z})\,\leq\,\frac{90\,dL^{4}}{\alpha^{4}N}\,\leq\,\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

Next, suppose we run the min-max gradient descent algorithm (
65
) from
\tilde{z}_{0}=(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with step size
\eta_{\mathsf{GD}}=\frac{\alpha}{4L^{2}}
for the number of iterations

k_{\mathsf{GD}}\geq\frac{4L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\frac{\alpha^{3}\|z^{*}\|^{2}}{\tau dL^{2}}
,
so that by Corollary
37
, we obtain a final point
m^{Z}:=\tilde{z}_{k_{\mathsf{GD}}}
which satisfies the guarantee:

\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}\leq\frac{\tau dL^{2}}{\alpha^{3}}.

We consider the Gaussian distribution
\gamma^{Z}:=\mathcal{N}(m^{Z},\frac{\tau}{L}I)
.
By the bound from Lemma
26
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\gamma^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{11dL^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}+\frac{L^{2}}{\tau\alpha}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}\,\leq\,\frac{12dL^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}

\displaystyle W_{2}(\gamma^{Z},\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{22\tau dL^{4}}{\alpha^{5}}+\frac{2L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\|m^{Z}-z^{*}\|^{2}\,\leq\,\frac{24\tau dL^{4}}{\alpha^{5}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\gamma^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}W_{2}(\gamma^{Z},\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}\,\leq\,\frac{12dL^{4}}{\alpha^{4}}+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}\cdot\frac{24\tau dL^{4}}{\alpha^{5}}\,\leq\,228\,\frac{dL^{6}}{\alpha^{6}}

where in the last inequality we use the bound
1\leq\frac{L}{\alpha}
to simplify the result, and
12+9\times 24=228
.
We use this to initialize the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) from the product distribution where each component is
\gamma^{Z}
:

\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}:=\left(\gamma^{Z}\right)^{\otimes N}.

This means we start the algorithm from
\mathbf{z}_{0}=(z_{0}^{1},\dots,z_{0}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}

where
z_{0}^{1},\dots,z_{0}^{N}\sim\gamma^{Z}
are i.i.d.
Since
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}=(\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{\otimes N}
is also a product distribution, the KL divergence and
W_{2}
distance split:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\left(\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{KL}(\gamma^{Z}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}W_{2}(\gamma^{Z},\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})^{2}\leq 228\,\frac{dL^{6}}{\alpha^{6}}.

Therefore, we can choose the number of iterations
k
of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) to be:

\displaystyle k\,\geq\,\frac{1}{\alpha\eta}\log\frac{3\cdot 228\,dL^{6}}{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{6}}\,=\,\frac{7500\,dL^{4}}{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{4}}\log\frac{684\,dL^{6}}{\varepsilon\,\alpha^{6}}

(55)

so that

\frac{e^{-\alpha\eta k}}{N}\left(\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})+\frac{9L^{2}}{\alpha\tau}W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

Combining all of the above, we conclude that if we run the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with the above choice of step size
\eta
and number of particles
N
, from the initial distribution
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\left(\gamma^{Z}\right)^{\otimes N}
,
then after
k
iterations given by (
55
), the average particle
z_{k}^{I}\sim\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}
satisfies, by Corollary
7
:

\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta,\mathrm{avg}}\,\|\,\bar{\nu}^{Z})\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}+\frac{\varepsilon}{3}=\varepsilon

as desired.
∎

Appendix G
Convergence of Finite-Particle Systems to Their Limiting Distributions

We study the convergence guarantees of the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) and algorithm (
20
) to their limiting stationary distributions.

G.1
Preliminary Results

G.1.1
Transformation of LSI constant

We recall the following classical result on how the LSI constant of a probability distribution changes under a pushforward operation by a Lipschitz map.

Lemma 27
.

(
Chafaï, 2004
, Remark 7)

Suppose
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{D})
satisfies
\alpha
-LSI for some
\alpha>0
.
Let
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{D}
be a differentiable map which is
M
-Lipschitz for some
0<M<\infty
.
Then the pushforward distribution
\tilde{\nu}=T_{\#}\nu
satisfies
(\alpha/M^{2})
-LSI.

We also recall the following result on how the LSI constant changes under a convolution.

Lemma 28
.

Suppose
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{D})
satisfies
\alpha
-LSI for some
\alpha>0
.
For
t>0
, the probability distribution
\nu_{t}:=\nu\ast\mathcal{N}(0,tI)
satisfies
\alpha_{t}
-LSI where
\alpha_{t}=(\frac{1}{\alpha}+t)^{-1}
.

Proof.

We recall by
(
Chafaï, 2004
, Corollary 3.1)
that if
\rho
satisfies
\alpha_{\rho}
-LSI and
\nu
satisfies
\alpha_{\nu}
-LSI, then the convolution
\rho\ast\nu
satisfies LSI with constant
(\frac{1}{\alpha_{\rho}}+\frac{1}{\alpha_{\nu}})^{-1}
.
By assumption,
\nu
satisfies
\alpha
-LSI.
Since the Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,tI)
is
(1/t)
-SLC, it satisfies
(1/t)
-LSI.
Then by the cited result above, the convolution
\nu_{t}=\nu\ast\mathcal{N}(0,tI)
satisfies LSI with constant
(\frac{1}{\alpha}+t)^{-1}
, as claimed.
∎

We recall the following property that KL divergence is preserved under a deterministic map.

Lemma 29
.

Let
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{D}
be a deterministic, differentiable bijective map.
For any probability distributions
\rho,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{D})
:

\mathsf{KL}(T_{\#}\rho\,\|\,T_{\#}\nu)=\mathsf{KL}(\rho\,\|\,\nu).

Proof.

This follows from a direct computation using the change-of-variable formula for
T_{\#}\rho
and
T_{\#}\nu
.
Alternatively, this follows from two applications of the data processing inequality from information theory, applied to the channels
T
and
T^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(T_{\#}\rho\,\|\,T_{\#}\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{KL}(\rho\,\|\,\nu)=\mathsf{KL}((T^{-1})_{\#}(T_{\#}\rho)\,\|\,(T^{-1})_{\#}(T_{\#}\nu))\leq\mathsf{KL}(T_{\#}\rho\,\|\,T_{\#}\nu).

Hence, both inequalities above must be equality.
∎

G.1.2
Contraction of the deterministic step of the finite-particle algorithm

The following shows that the deterministic step in the finite-particle algorithm is a contraction.

Lemma 30
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
For
\eta>0
, define the map
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
by, for
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
:

G(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}+\eta b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})

where
b^{\mathbf{Z}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2dN}\to\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
is the vector field defined in (
17
).
If
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}
, then
G
is
M
-Lipschitz, where:

M:=\sqrt{1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2}}\in[0,1].

Proof.

For any
\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
, we can compute:

\displaystyle\left\|G(\mathbf{z})-G(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}+2\eta\left\langle\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}},b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\rangle+\eta^{2}\left\|b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\bar{\mathbf{z}})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}-2\eta\alpha\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}+4\eta^{2}L^{2}\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2})\left\|\mathbf{z}-\bar{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}.

In the above, in the first step we expand the square.
In the second step, we use the properties from Lemma
12
that
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
(2L)
-Lipschitz, and
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
In the last step, we collect the terms.
Note that
1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2}\in[0,1]
from our assumption that
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}
.
∎

G.1.3
Bounds on the second moment along the finite-particle systems

In this section, we show that along the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) and algorithm (
20
), the distributions remain in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.

Under Assumption
1
, recall from Theorem
35
(in Section
H
) that there exists a unique equilibrium point
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
which satisfies
\nabla V(z^{*})=0
.
Define
\mathbf{z}^{*}=(x^{*},\dots,x^{*},y^{*},\dots,y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
Then by construction,
b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}^{*})=0
, where
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is the vector field defined in (
17
).

Lemma 31
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
from
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
Then
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

We note that
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
by virtue of the Brownian motion component in the dynamics (
16
).
We now show that the second moment of
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
remains finite for all
t\geq 0
.
Since
\mathbf{Z}_{t}
evolves following the dynamics (
16
), its density
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the Fokker-Planck equation:

\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\tau\,\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}.

From this, we can compute, using integration by parts:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})}{\partial t}\,\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}\,d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\left(-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)(\mathbf{z})+\tau\,\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\right)\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}\,d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\left\langle b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}),\nabla\left(\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}\right)\right\rangle\,d\mathbf{z}+\tau\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\,\Delta\left(\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}\right)\,d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\left\langle b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}),\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rangle\,d\mathbf{z}+\tau\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\,(4dN)\,d\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\left\langle b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}^{*}),\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\rangle\,d\mathbf{z}+4\tau dN

\displaystyle\leq-2\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2dN}}\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})\left\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\,d\mathbf{z}+4\tau dN

\displaystyle=-2\alpha\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+4\tau dN

where the inequality above uses the property that
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, from Lemma
12
.
We can write the differential inequality above equivalently as:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(e^{2\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\right)\leq e^{2\alpha t}\,4\tau dN.

Integrating from
0
to
t
and rearranging the result gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{(1-e^{-2\alpha t})}{\alpha}\,2\tau dN

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{2\tau dN}{\alpha}.

Since
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
,
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]<\infty
, so
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]+2\|\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}<\infty
.
Therefore, we also have for all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+2\|\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{4\tau dN}{\alpha}+2\|\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}\,<\,\infty

which shows that
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
∎

Lemma 32
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Suppose
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
evolves via the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
0<\eta<\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}
from
\mathbf{z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
Then
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
for all
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

We note that
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
since the update rule of the algorithm (
20
) adds an independent Gaussian random variable, which corresponds to convolution with the Gaussian distribution.
We now show that the second moment of
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
remains finite for all
k\geq 0
.

Define
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by
G(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}+\eta b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})
.
By Lemma
30
, we know
G
is
M
-Lipschitz where
M=\sqrt{1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2}},

and note that
0<M<1
from the assumption
\eta<\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}
.
Note also that by definition,
G(\mathbf{z}^{*})=\mathbf{z}^{*}
since
b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}^{*})=0
.
We have the update rule from (
20
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}_{k}+\eta b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z}_{k})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{z}}=G(\mathbf{z}_{k})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{z}}

where
\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
is independent of
\mathbf{z}_{k}
.
Then we can compute:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|G(\mathbf{z}_{k})-G(\mathbf{z}^{*})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|G(\mathbf{z}_{k})-G(\mathbf{z}^{*})\right\|^{2}\right]+2\tau\eta\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\bm{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathbf{z}}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq M^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{k}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+4\tau\eta dN

where the inequality follows from the property that
G
is
M
-Lipschitz.
Since
0<M<1
, we can iterate the recurrence above to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{k}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq M^{2k}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+4\tau\eta dN\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}M^{2i}\,\leq\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{4\tau\eta dN}{1-M^{2}}.

Since
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
,
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]<\infty
, so
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]+2\|\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}<~\infty
.
Therefore, we also have for all
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{k}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+2\|\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}}\left[\left\|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\mathbf{z}^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{8\tau\eta dN}{1-M^{2}}+2\|\mathbf{z}^{*}\|^{2}\,<\,\infty

which shows that
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
∎

G.2
Convergence of Finite-Particle Dynamics to Its Stationary Distribution

We show the following exponential convergence rates of the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) to its stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
in
W_{2}
distance and in KL divergence.
We note that the exponential convergence rate in KL divergence guarantee below can be strengthened to hold for all Rényi divergence, but we only present the result for KL divergence here for simplicity.

Theorem 33
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
There exists a unique stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
of the finite-particle dynamics (
16
), and it satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-LSI.
Furthermore, suppose
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolves following the finite-particle dynamics (
16
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
from
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
For all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}})^{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}).

Proof.

We showed in Lemma
31
that since
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
, we have
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
for all
t\geq 0
.

(1) Exponential contraction in
W_{2}
distance:

Suppose we run two copies of the finite-particle dynamics (
20
) from
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, where
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{0})
has the joint distribution which is the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
,
to get
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, for
t\geq 0
.
We can write the two stochastic processes using synchronous coupling:

\displaystyle d\mathbf{Z}_{t}

\displaystyle=b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle d\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}

\displaystyle=b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\,dt+\sqrt{2\tau}\,dW_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

we use the same standard Brownian motion
(W_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}
.
Then we have:

\displaystyle d(\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})=\left(b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right)dt

where the Brownian motion terms cancel because they are equal in the two processes.
From this, we get:

\displaystyle d\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}=2\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle dt.

Taking expectation over the joint distribution, we obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t},\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{Z}_{t})-b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t})\right\rangle\right]\,\leq\,-2\alpha\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]

where in the last step we use the property from Lemma
12
that
-b^{\mathbf{Z}}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
Integrating the differential inequality above from
0
to
t
, and using the fact that
(\mathbf{Z}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{0})
has the optimal
W_{2}
coupling gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{t}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right]\,\leq\,e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\mathbf{Z}_{0}-\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\right\|^{2}\right]\,=\,e^{-2\alpha t}\,W_{2}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{2}.

Using the definition of the
W_{2}
distance as the infimum over all coupling, we conclude that

\displaystyle W_{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\,\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{2}\,\leq\,e^{-2\alpha t}\,W_{2}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{2}.

This implies
\lim_{t\to\infty}W_{2}\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}},\,\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{2}=0
.
Since this holds for any initial distributions
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}},\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, this shows that there must be a stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
of the dynamics, and it is unique by the contraction property above.
Then plugging in
\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
yields the desired exponential convergence guarantee in
W_{2}
distance.

(2) Isoperimetry of the stationary distribution:

As
\eta\to 0
, the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
\eta
recovers the continuous-time finite-particle dynamics (
16
).
In particular, as
\eta\to 0
, the stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
of the finite-particle algorithm recovers the stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
of the finite-particle dynamics.
We show in Theorem
34
below that
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies
\alpha_{\eta}=\frac{(\alpha-2\eta L^{2})}{\tau}
-LSI, which implies
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies LSI with constant
\lim_{\eta\to 0}\alpha_{\eta}=\alpha/\tau
, as claimed.

(3) Exponential convergence in KL divergence:

We run two copies of the finite-particle dynamics (
20
) from
\mathbf{Z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and from the stationary distribution
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{0}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
,
to get
\mathbf{Z}_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Z}}_{t}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, for
t\geq 0
.
Then
\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}
evolve following the Fokker-Planck equations:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\tau\,\Delta\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\cdot\left(\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,b^{\mathbf{Z}}\right)+\tau\,\Delta\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}.

Using the identity from Lemma
19
, we can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle=-\tau\,\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})\,\leq\,-2\alpha\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})

where in the second step we use the fact that
\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
satisfies
(\alpha/\tau)
-LSI.
Integrating the differential inequality above from
0
to
t
yields:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}})\,\leq\,e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}).

Substituting
