\tilde{\rho}_{t}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{Z}}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
yields the desired exponential convergence rate in KL divergence.
∎

G.3
Convergence of Finite-Particle Algorithm to Its Stationary Distribution

We show the following exponential convergence rates of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) to its stationary limiting distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.
We note that the convergence in KL divergence below can be strengthened to be an exponential convergence in all Rényi divergence, similar to the result for the Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm shown in
(
Vempala and Wibisono, 2019
)
; for simplicity, here we only present the convergence results for
W_{2}
distance and KL divergence.

We also note that similar to the standard discretization of the Langevin dynamics as the Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm which is biased, here the stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) is not equal to the stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
of the continuous-time finite-particle dynamics (
16
).
We can also characterize the biased convergence of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) to the continuous-time stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, but we skip this part because in principle we do not actually care about the convergence to
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, but only about the biased convergence to the stationary mean-field distribution
\bar{\nu}^{\mathbf{Z}}
, which we show in Theorem
6
in the main text of this paper.

Theorem 34
.

Assume Assumption
1
and
0<\eta<\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}
.
Then there exists a unique stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
\eta
, and
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies
\alpha_{\eta}
-LSI where

\alpha_{\eta}=(\alpha-2\eta L^{2})/\tau
.
Furthermore, suppose
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
evolves following the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
0<\eta<\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}
from
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
.
Define
M=\sqrt{1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2}}\in(0,1)
.
Then for all
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M^{2k}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})^{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})

\displaystyle\leq M^{2k}\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}).

Proof.

We showed in Lemma
32
that since
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
, we have
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2dN})
for all
k\geq 0
.

Define
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by
G(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}+\eta b^{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{z})
.
By Lemma
30
, we know
G
is
M
-Lipschitz where

M=\sqrt{1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2}},

and note that
0<M<1
from the assumption
\eta<\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}
.

(1) Exponential contraction in
W_{2}
distance:

Suppose we run two copies of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
0<\eta<\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}
from
\mathbf{z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{0}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
, where
(\mathbf{z}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{0})
has the joint distribution which is the optimal
W_{2}
coupling between
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
,
to get
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
, for
k\geq 1
.
We can write the update of the algorithm (
20
) using synchronous coupling as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=G(\mathbf{z}_{k})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k},

(56a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=G(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k})+\sqrt{2\tau\eta}\,\bm{\zeta}_{k}

(56b)

where we use the same Gaussian noise
\bm{\zeta}_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
for both updates, independent of
\mathbf{z}_{k}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}
.
Then we can compute:

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k+1}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|G(\mathbf{z}_{k})-G(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k})\|^{2}\,\leq\,M^{2}\|\mathbf{z}_{k}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}\|^{2}

where in the last step we have used the property that
G
is
M
-Lipschitz, from Lemma
30
.
Taking expectation over the joint distribution, this gives:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{z}_{k+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k+1}\|^{2}\right]\,\leq\,M^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{z}_{k}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}\|^{2}\right].

Unrolling the recursion and using the fact that
(\mathbf{z}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{0})
has the optimal
W_{2}
coupling, we get:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{z}_{k}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}\|^{2}\right]\,\leq\,M^{2k}\,\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbf{z}_{0}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{0}\|^{2}\right]\,=\,M^{2k}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})^{2}.

Using the definition of the
W_{2}
distance as the infimum over all coupling, this implies:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\,\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})^{2}\,\leq\,M^{2k}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})^{2}.

Since
0<M<1
, this implies
\lim_{k\to\infty}W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\,\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})^{2}=0
.
Since this holds for any initial distributions
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
, this shows that there must be a stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
of the algorithm, and it is unique by the contraction property above.
Then plugging in
\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
gives us the desired exponential convergence guarantee in
W_{2}
distance.

(2) Isoperimetry of the stationary distribution:

We write each step of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) as a composition of a pushforward of the deterministic map
G
, followed by a Gaussian convolution, so at each iteration
k\geq 0
, the next distribution is given by:

\displaystyle\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=(G_{\#}\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta})\ast\mathcal{N}(0,2\tau\eta I).

(57)

Suppose we run the algorithm (
20
) from
\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
which satisfies
\beta_{0}
-LSI for some
0<\beta_{0}<\infty

(for example, a Gaussian distribution).
Then inductively from the iteration above, for each
k\geq 1
,
\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies
\beta_{k}
-LSI, where the constants
(\beta_{k})_{k\geq 0}
satisfy the recurrence:

\displaystyle\beta_{k+1}=\frac{1}{\frac{M^{2}}{\beta_{k}}+2\tau\eta}=\frac{\beta_{k}}{M^{2}+2\tau\eta\beta_{k}}.

(58)

Define
\alpha_{\eta}
by:

\alpha_{\eta}:=\frac{1-M^{2}}{2\tau\eta}=\frac{\alpha-2\eta L^{2}}{\tau}

and observe that
\beta_{k}=\alpha_{\eta}
is a fixed point of the recurrence (
58
).
Furthermore, we can rewrite the recurrence (
58
) as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta_{k+1}}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{\eta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{M^{2}}{\beta_{k}}+\frac{2\tau\eta\alpha_{\eta}-1}{\alpha_{\eta}}\,=\,\frac{M^{2}}{\beta_{k}}-\frac{M^{2}}{\alpha_{\eta}}\,=\,M^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{k}}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{\eta}}\right).

Unrolling the recurrence, we get:

\frac{1}{\beta_{k}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\eta}}+M^{2k}\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{\eta}}\right).

As
M^{2}<1
, this shows that
1/\beta_{k}
converges to
1/\alpha_{\eta}
exponentially fast.
Therefore, the stationary distribution
\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies LSI with constant
\lim_{k\to\infty}\beta_{k}=\alpha_{\eta}
, as desired.

(3) Exponential convergence in KL divergence:

We run two copies of the finite-particle algorithm (
20
) with step size
0<\eta<\frac{\alpha}{2L^{2}}
from
\mathbf{z}_{0}\sim\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and from the stationary distribution
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{0}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
, to get
\mathbf{z}_{k}\sim\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{k}\sim\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.
We write one step of the algorithm update as a pushforward followed by a Gaussian convolution, as in (
57
).
We define the half-steps:

\displaystyle\rho_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}

\displaystyle=G_{\#}\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta},\qquad\qquad\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=G_{\#}\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}.

Since
G
is a contraction (
M
-Lipschitz with
M<1
), it is bijective, and so we know by Lemma
29
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\big\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)\,=\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right).

Furthermore, since
\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies
\alpha_{\eta}
-LSI, and
G
is
M
-Lipschitz, we know by Lemma
27
that
\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies
(\alpha_{\eta}/M^{2})
-LSI.
Then we interpret the next half-step, the Gaussian convolution:

\displaystyle\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}

\displaystyle=\rho_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\ast\mathcal{N}(0,2\tau\eta I),\qquad\qquad\tilde{\rho}_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\ast\mathcal{N}(0,2\tau\eta I),

as the solutions of the heat flow:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{t}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\Delta\rho_{t},\qquad\qquad\frac{\partial\nu_{t}}{\partial t}=\Delta\nu_{t}

starting from
\rho_{0}:=\rho_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\nu_{0}:=\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
,
for time
t=\tau\eta
, to get

\rho_{\tau\eta}=\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
and
\nu_{\tau\eta}=\tilde{\rho}_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
.
Note since
\nu_{0}=\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
satisfies
\gamma_{0}
-LSI with
\gamma_{0}:=(\alpha_{\eta}/M^{2})
, by Lemma
28
, we know
\nu_{t}=\nu_{0}\ast\mathcal{N}(0,2tI)
satisfies
\gamma_{t}
-LSI where:

\gamma_{t}=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\gamma_{0}}+2t}=\frac{\gamma_{0}}{1+2\gamma_{0}\,t}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\log(1+2\gamma_{0}\,t).

Then by the formula from Lemma
19
, we can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\nu_{t})

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{FI}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\nu_{t})\,\leq\,-2\gamma_{t}\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{t}\,\|\,\nu_{t}).

Integrating from
t=0
to
t=\tau\eta
gives:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{\tau\eta}\,\|\,\nu_{\tau\eta})

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-2\int_{0}^{\tau\eta}\gamma_{t}\,dt\right)\,\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}\,\|\,\nu_{0})\,=\,\frac{\mathsf{KL}(\rho_{0}\,\|\,\nu_{0})}{1+2\gamma_{0}\tau\eta}.

Note that by definition,

\displaystyle 1+2\gamma_{0}\tau\eta\,=\,1+\frac{2\alpha_{\eta}\tau\eta}{M^{2}}\,=\,1+\frac{1-M^{2}}{M^{2}}\,=\,\frac{1}{M^{2}}.

Then substituting back the definitions of
\rho_{\tau\eta},\rho_{0}
, and
\nu_{\tau\eta},\nu_{0}
gives us:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\big\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)}{1+2\gamma_{0}\tau\eta}\,=\,M^{2}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\big\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{k+\frac{1}{2}}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)\,=\,M^{2}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right).

Iterating this recursion and substituting
\tilde{\rho}_{k+1}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\tilde{\rho}_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}=\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}
gives:

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{k}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)\,\leq\,M^{2k}\,\mathsf{KL}\left(\rho_{0}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\,\|\,\rho_{\infty}^{\mathbf{z},\eta}\right)

as desired.
∎

Appendix H
Deterministic Zero-Sum Game

We review the properties of the deterministic finite-dimensional zero-sum game:

\displaystyle\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\,V(x,y)

(59)

where
V\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies Assumption
1
.
In particular, for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x,y)<\infty
, and similarly, for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x,y)>-\infty
,
so all the quantities we discuss below are finite.

Recall we say that
(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
is an
equilibrium point
(or a
Nash equilibrium
) for the game (
59
) if for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle V(x^{*},y)\leq V(x^{*},y^{*})\leq V(x,y^{*}).

(60)

We recall the
duality gap

\mathsf{DG}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
of the game (
59
) is defined by:

\mathsf{DG}(x,y)=\max_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x,y^{\prime})-\min_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x^{\prime},y).

Note that
\mathsf{DG}(x,y)\geq 0
for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, and
\mathsf{DG}(x,y)=0
if and only if
(x,y)=(x^{*},y^{*})
is an equilibrium point.
Under Assumption
1
, there exists a unique equilibrium point
(x^{*},y^{*})
; furthermore, the duality gap is bounded by the squared gradient norm, see Theorem
35
below.

In this section, we review the exponential convergence guarantees of the continuous-time
min-max gradient flow
in Section
H.1
, and the discrete-time
min-max gradient descent
in Section
H.2
.

In Section
I
, we review the zero-sum game (
2
) in the space of distributions without entropy regularization.
Under the same assumptions on
V
, we show that the unique equilibrium distribution of the game (
2
) is a pure equilibrium, i.e., a point mass
(\delta_{x^{*}},\delta_{y^{*}})
, where
(x^{*},y^{*})
is the equilibrium point of the deterministic game from this section.

H.1
Convergence of Min-Max Gradient Flow

We consider the
min-max gradient flow
which is the dynamics for
(X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
which evolves following:

\displaystyle\dot{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})

\displaystyle\dot{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{y}V(X_{t},Y_{t}).

In terms of the joint variable
Z_{t}=(X_{t},Y_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, we can write this as:

\displaystyle\dot{Z}_{t}=b^{Z}(Z_{t})

(61)

where we have defined the vector field
b^{Z}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
by, for all
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
:

\displaystyle b^{Z}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\\
\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\end{pmatrix}.

(62)

Observe that
b^{Z}
is the case
N=1
of the vector field
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
that we defined in (
17
) (in
\mathbb{R}^{2dN}=\mathbb{R}^{2d}
).
In particular, the guarantees that we proved for
b^{\mathbf{Z}}
also apply to
b^{Z}
when we specialize to
N=1
.

Below, for
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, we also write
\nabla V(z)\equiv\nabla V(x,y)
, and
\mathsf{DG}(z)\equiv\mathsf{DG}(x,y)
.

Theorem 35
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Then we have the following properties:

1.

There exists a unique equilibrium point
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, and it satisfies
\nabla V(z^{*})=0
.

2.

For all
z=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, the duality gap is bounded by the squared gradient norm:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DG}(z)\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}\|\nabla V(z)\|^{2}.

(63)

3.

Suppose
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
evolves following the min-max gradient flow (
61
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
For all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\|Z_{t}-z^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\|Z_{0}-z^{*}\|^{2}

(64a)

\displaystyle 2\alpha\,\mathsf{DG}(Z_{t})\leq\|\nabla V(Z_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\|\nabla V(Z_{0})\|^{2}

(64b)

Proof.

(1) Bound in duality gap:

For each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since
x\mapsto V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly convex by assumption, it also satisfies
\alpha
-gradient domination, i.e.,

V(x,y)-\min_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x^{\prime},y)\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}\|\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\|^{2}.

Similarly, for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since
y\mapsto-V(x,y)
is
\alpha
-strongly convex by assumption, it also satisfies
\alpha
-gradient domination, i.e.,

\max_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x,y^{\prime})-V(x,y)\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}\|\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\|^{2}.

Summing the two inequalities above gives:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DG}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\max_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x,y^{\prime})-\min_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x^{\prime},y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\alpha}\left(\|\nabla_{x}V(x,y)\|^{2}+\|\nabla_{y}V(x,y)\|^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2\alpha}\|\nabla V(x,y)\|^{2}

as claimed in (
63
).

(2) Exponential convergence in distance and existence of stationary point:

Suppose we run two copies of the min-max gradient flow (
61
) from
Z_{0},Z_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, to get
Z_{t},Z_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
for
t\geq 0
,
so they satisfy:
\dot{Z}_{t}=b^{Z}(Z_{t})
and
\dot{Z}_{t}^{\prime}=b^{Z}(Z_{t}^{\prime})
.
Then we can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|Z_{t}-Z_{t}^{\prime}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\langle Z_{t}-Z_{t}^{\prime},b(Z_{t})-b(Z_{t}^{\prime})\rangle\leq-2\alpha\|Z_{t}-Z_{t}^{\prime}\|^{2}

where the inequality follows from the property that
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone, by Lemma
12
.
Integrating the differential inequality above gives:

\displaystyle\|Z_{t}-Z_{t}^{\prime}\|^{2}\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\|Z_{0}-Z_{0}^{\prime}\|^{2}.

Therefore,
\lim_{t\to\infty}\|Z_{t}-Z_{t}^{\prime}\|^{2}=0
.
Since this holds for any initial points
Z_{0},Z_{0}^{\prime}
, this means there must be a stationary point
z^{*}
, and furthermore, this stationary point
z^{*}
is unique by the contraction property above.
Plugging in
Z_{t}^{\prime}=Z_{0}^{\prime}=z^{*}
to the guarantee above gives the desired exponential convergence rate in distance (
64a
).

(3) Stationary point is an equilibrium point:

Since
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})
is stationary for the min-max gradient flow (
61
), it makes the vector field vanish:
b^{Z}(z^{*})=0
, which means
\nabla_{x}V(x^{*},y^{*})=0
and
\nabla_{y}V(x^{*},y^{*})=0
, so indeed
\nabla V(x^{*},y^{*})=0
.

Furthermore, since
x\mapsto V(x,y^{*})

is strongly convex by assumption,
\nabla_{x}V(x^{*},y^{*})=0
means
x^{*}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x,y^{*})
, so
V(x^{*},y^{*})\leq V(x,y^{*})
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Similarly, since
y\mapsto V(x^{*},y)
is strongly concave by assumption,
\nabla_{y}V(x^{*},y^{*})=0
means
y^{*}=\arg\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}V(x^{*},y)
, so
V(x^{*},y^{*})\geq V(x^{*},y)
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
This shows that
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})
is an equilibrium point as defined in (
60
).

(4) Exponential convergence in gradient norm:

Observe that
\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}=\|\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})\|^{2}+\|\nabla_{y}V(X_{t},Y_{t})\|^{2}=\|\nabla V(X_{t},Y_{t})\|^{2}
,
so it suffices to prove the exponential convergence of
\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|
.
We can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t})\,\dot{Z}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t})\,b^{Z}(Z_{t})\rangle

\displaystyle=2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),(\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t}))_{\mathrm{sym}}\,b^{Z}(Z_{t})\rangle

where the last step follows since
u^{\top}Au=u^{\top}(A_{\mathrm{sym}})u
for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and
A\in\mathbb{R}^{2d\times 2d}
, where
A_{\mathrm{sym}}=\frac{1}{2}(A+A^{\top})
.
Recall from Lemma
12
part (2), we have shown that
(\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t}))_{\mathrm{sym}}\preceq-\alpha I
(which is equivalent to the property that
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone).
Then from the computation above, we can bound:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-2\alpha\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}.

Integrating this differential inequality gives:

\displaystyle\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|^{2}

as claimed in the second inequality in (
64b
).
Combining this with the bound for the duality gap (
63
) yields:

\displaystyle 2\alpha\,\mathsf{DG}(Z_{t})\leq\|\nabla V(Z_{t})\|^{2}\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\|\nabla V(Z_{0})\|^{2}

as desired.
∎

H.2
Convergence of Min-Max Gradient Descent

We consider the
min-max gradient descent
with step size
\eta>0
, which maintains the iterates
(x_{k},y_{k})_{k\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
which evolves following the update:

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k}-\eta\nabla_{x}V(x_{k},y_{k})

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}+\eta\nabla_{y}V(x_{k},y_{k}).

In terms of the joint variable
z_{k}=(x_{k},y_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, we can write this as:

\displaystyle z_{k+1}=z_{k}+\eta b^{Z}(z_{k})

(65)

where
b^{Z}
is the vector field defined in (
62
).

Theorem 36
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Let
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be the unique equilibrium point.
Suppose
(z_{k})_{k\geq 0}
evolves following the min-max gradient descent (
65
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with step size
\eta>0
.
If
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{4L^{2}}
, then for all
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\|z_{k}-z^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\|z_{0}-z^{*}\|^{2}.

(66)

Furthermore, if
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{16L^{2}}
, then we also have for all
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle 2\alpha\,\mathsf{DG}(z_{k})\,\leq\,\|\nabla V(z_{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\|\nabla V(z_{0})\|^{2}.

(67)

Proof.

(1) Exponential convergence in distance:
Let
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be
G(\mathbf{z})=\mathbf{z}+\eta b^{Z}(\mathbf{z})
.
Recall we show in Lemma
30
(with
N=1
) that
G
is
M
-Lipschitz, where

\displaystyle M:=\sqrt{1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2}}\leq\sqrt{1-\eta\alpha}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\eta\alpha}

where the first bound above follows from the assumption
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{4L^{2}}
, and the second from the inequality
1-c\leq e^{-c}
for
c\geq 0
.
We can write the min-max gradient descent update (
65
) as
z_{k+1}=G(z_{k})
, and note
z^{*}=G(z^{*})
is a fixed point.
Then we can compute:

\displaystyle\|z_{k+1}-z^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|G(z_{k})-G(z^{*})\|^{2}\leq M^{2}\,\|z_{k}-z^{*}\|^{2}

where the inequality follows from the property that
G
is
M
-Lipschitz.
Iterating this bound gives:

\displaystyle\|z_{k}-z^{*}\|^{2}\,\leq\,M^{2k}\,\|z_{0}-z^{*}\|^{2}\,\leq\,e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\|z_{0}-z^{*}\|^{2}

where the last inequality follows from the bound for
M
above.

(2) Exponential convergence in gradient norm:
We consider a continuous-time interpolation of one step of the min-max gradient descent (
65
) as:

Z_{t}=Z_{0}+tb^{Z}(Z_{0})

so that if
Z_{0}=z_{k}
, then
Z_{\eta}=Z_{0}+\eta b^{Z}(Z_{0})=z_{k}+\eta b^{Z}(z_{k})=z_{k+1}
.
We can first bound:

\displaystyle\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})-b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4L^{2}\,\|Z_{t}-Z_{0}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\,4t^{2}L^{2}\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 8t^{2}L^{2}\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})-b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|^{2}+8t^{2}L^{2}\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})-b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|^{2}+8t^{2}L^{2}\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}

where the first inequality follows from the property that
b^{Z}
is
(2L)
-Lipschitz from Lemma
12
.
In the second inequality we introduce the term
b^{Z}(Z_{t})
and use the inequality
\|a+b\|^{2}\leq 2\|a\|^{2}+2\|b\|^{2}
.
In the third inequality we use the assumption
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{16L^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{4L}
, so
8t^{2}L^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
Rearranging the above and taking square-root gives us:

\displaystyle\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})-b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|

\displaystyle\leq 4tL\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|.

(68)

Next, we can compute along the continuous-time interpolation, where
\dot{Z}_{t}=b^{Z}(Z_{0})
, for
0\leq t\leq\eta
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t})\,b^{Z}(Z_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t})\,b^{Z}(Z_{t})\rangle+2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t})\,(b^{Z}(Z_{0})-b^{Z}(Z_{t}))\rangle

\displaystyle=2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),(\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t}))_{\mathrm{sym}}\,b^{Z}(Z_{t})\rangle+2\langle b^{Z}(Z_{t}),\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t})\,(b^{Z}(Z_{0})-b^{Z}(Z_{t}))\rangle

\displaystyle\leq-2\alpha\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}+4L\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|\cdot\|b^{Z}(Z_{0})-b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|

\displaystyle\leq-2\alpha\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}+16tL^{2}\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\alpha\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})\|^{2}.

In the first inequality above, we use the properties from Lemma
12
that
(\nabla b^{Z}(Z_{t}))_{\mathrm{sym}}\preceq-\alpha I
(equivalent to
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone), and
b^{Z}
is
(2L)
-Lipschitz.
In the second inequality, we use the bound on
\|b^{Z}(Z_{t})-b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|
from (
68
).
In the third inequality, we use the assumption
t\leq\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{16L^{2}}
, so
16tL^{2}\leq\alpha
.
Integrating the differential inequality above from
t=0
to
t=\eta
gives:

\displaystyle\|b^{Z}(z_{k+1})\|^{2}=\|b^{Z}(Z_{\eta})\|^{2}\leq e^{-\alpha\eta}\,\|b^{Z}(Z_{0})\|^{2}=e^{-\alpha\eta}\,\|b^{Z}(z_{k})\|^{2}.

Iterating the recurrence above and recalling
\|b^{Z}(z)\|=\|\nabla V(z)\|
gives us:

\displaystyle\|\nabla V(z_{k})\|^{2}=\|b^{Z}(z_{k})\|^{2}\,\leq\,e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\|b^{Z}(z_{0})\|^{2}=e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\|\nabla V(z_{0})\|^{2}

as claimed in the second inequality in (
67
).
Finally, combining this bound with the bound for the duality gap (
63
) yields:

\displaystyle 2\alpha\,\mathsf{DG}(z_{k})\leq\|\nabla V(z_{k})\|^{2}\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\|\nabla V(z_{0})\|^{2}

as desired.
∎

H.2.1
Corollary on iteration complexity of min-max gradient descent

Corollary 37
.

Given any
\varepsilon>0
, if we run the min-max gradient descent algorithm (
65
)
from
z_{0}=(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with step size
\eta=\frac{\alpha}{4L^{2}}
, then we have

\|z_{k}-z^{*}\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon

for all

k\geq\frac{4L^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\|z^{*}\|^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right).

Proof.

This follows from the bound (
66
) from Theorem
36
:

\displaystyle\|z_{k}-z^{*}\|^{2}\leq\exp\left(-\alpha\eta k\right)\|z^{*}\|^{2}=\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha^{2}k}{4L^{2}}\right)\|z^{*}\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon

where the last inequality follows from our choice of
k
.
∎

Appendix I
Zero-Sum Game in the Space of Distributions Without Regularization

Consider the zero-sum game in the space of probability distributions without entropy regularization:

\displaystyle\min_{\rho^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\,\max_{\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\;\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\rho^{Y}}[V]

(69)

where
V\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies Assumption
1
.
This is the same game (
2
) as in Section
1
.

We say that a pair of probability distributions
(\bar{\nu}^{X},\bar{\nu}^{Y})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an
equilibrium distribution
for the game (
69
) if the following holds for all
(\rho^{X},\rho^{Y})\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\rho^{Y}}[V]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\bar{\nu}^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[V]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\bar{\nu}^{Y}}[V].

(70)

Under Assumption
1
, there exists a unique equilibrium distribution, which is the point mass
(\delta_{x^{*}},\delta_{y^{*}})
, where
(x^{*},y^{*})
is the equilibrium point of the deterministic game (
59
), see Theorem
38
below.

We consider the
mean-field min-max gradient flow
which is the dynamics for random variables
Z_{t}=(X_{t},Y_{t})\sim\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}=\rho_{t}^{Z}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
which evolves via:

\displaystyle\dot{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})]

(71a)

\displaystyle\dot{Y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{X}}[\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})].

(71b)

We also consider the
mean-field min-max gradient descent
algorithm with step size
\eta>0
which maintains random variables
z_{k}=(x_{k},y_{k})\sim\rho_{k}^{x,\eta}\otimes\rho_{k}^{y,\eta}=\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with the update rule:

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=x_{k}-\eta\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{y,\eta}}[\nabla_{x}V(x_{k},y_{k})]

(72a)

\displaystyle y_{k+1}

\displaystyle=y_{k}+\eta\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{x,\eta}}[\nabla_{y}V(x_{k},y_{k})].

(72b)

We have the following convergence guarantees.

Theorem 38
.

Assume Assumption
1
.
Let
z^{*}=(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be the unique equilibrium point for the deterministic game (
59
).
Then:

1.

There exists a unique equilibrium distribution for the game (
69
), which is the point mass distribution:
(\delta_{x^{*}},\delta_{y^{*}})
.

2.

Suppose
Z_{t}=(X_{t},Y_{t})\sim\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}=\rho_{t}^{Z}
evolves via the mean-field min-max gradient flow (
71
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
,
and let
\delta_{z^{*}}=\delta_{x^{*}}\otimes\delta_{y^{*}}
.
For all
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{Z},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{Z},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}.

(73)

3.

Suppose
z_{k}=(x_{k},y_{k})\sim\rho_{k}^{x,\eta}\otimes\rho_{k}^{y,\eta}=\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}
evolves following the mean-field min-max gradient descent (
72
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with step size
0<\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{4L^{2}}
.
Then for all
k\geq 0
:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{z,\eta},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}.

(74)

Proof.

(1) Equilibrium distribution:

Recall since
(x^{*},y^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
is an equilibrium point for the deterministic game (
59
), it satisfies the property (
60
) that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle V(x^{*},y)\leq V(x^{*},y^{*})\leq V(x,y^{*}).

Then for any probability distributions
\rho^{X},\rho^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
X\sim\rho^{X}
and
Y\sim\rho^{Y}
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{Y}}[V(x^{*},Y)]\leq V(x^{*},y^{*})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}}[V(X,y^{*})].

We can write this equivalently as the condition (
70
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{x^{*}}\otimes\rho^{Y}}[V]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\delta_{x^{*}}\otimes\delta_{y^{*}}}[V]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho^{X}\otimes\delta_{y^{*}}}[V].

This shows that
(\delta_{x^{*}},\delta_{y^{*}})
is an equilibrium distribution of the game (
59
).

Furthermore, note that any equilibrium distribution of the game (
59
) is a stationary distribution for the mean-field min-max gradient flow (
71
).
Then the uniqueness of the equilibrium follows from the convergence guarantee (
73
) that we will prove below, which shows that along the mean-field min-max gradient flow (
71
), any starting distribution
\rho_{0}^{Z}
converges to
\delta_{z^{*}}=\delta_{x^{*}}\otimes\delta_{y^{*}}
.

(2) Convergence of mean-field min-max gradient flow:
Let
b^{Z}\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be the vector field defined in (
62
).
Recall from Lemma
12
that
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
Recall that
b^{Z}(z^{*})=0
.

Let
Z_{t}=(X_{t},Y_{t})\sim\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}=\rho_{t}^{Z}
evolve via the mean-field min-max gradient flow (
71
) in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
.
Then we can compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|X_{t}-x^{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\langle X_{t}-x^{*},\dot{X}_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\left\langle X_{t}-x^{*},\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Y}}[\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})]\right\rangle

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Y}}\big[\left\langle X_{t}-x^{*},\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})\right\rangle\big].

Taking expectation over
X_{t}\sim\rho_{t}^{X}
and writing
\rho_{t}^{Z}=\rho_{t}^{X}\otimes\rho_{t}^{Y}
gives:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{X}}\left[\|X_{t}-x^{*}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\big[\left\langle X_{t}-x^{*},\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})\right\rangle\big].

Similarly, we can also compute:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Y}}\left[\|Y_{t}-y^{*}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\big[\left\langle Y_{t}-y^{*},\nabla_{y}V(X_{t},Y_{t})\right\rangle\big].

Adding the two identities above gives:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|Z_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{X}}\left[\|X_{t}-x^{*}\|^{2}\right]+\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Y}}\left[\|Y_{t}-y^{*}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\big[\left(\left\langle X_{t}-x^{*},-\nabla_{x}V(X_{t},Y_{t})\right\rangle+\left\langle Y_{t}-y^{*},\nabla_{y}V(X_{t},Y_{t})\right\rangle\right)\big]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\big[\left\langle Z_{t}-z^{*},b^{Z}(Z_{t})\right\rangle\big]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\big[\left\langle Z_{t}-z^{*},b^{Z}(Z_{t})-b^{Z}(z^{*})\right\rangle\big]

\displaystyle\leq-2\alpha\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|Z_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

where the inequality follows from the property that
-b^{Z}
is
\alpha
-strongly monotone.
Integrating the differential inequality above from
0
to
t
, and noting the special formula for the
W_{2}
distance to a point mass
\delta_{z^{*}}
yields the desired convergence guarantee:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{t}^{Z},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{t}^{Z}}\left[\left\|Z_{t}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq e^{-2\alpha t}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{Z}}\left[\left\|Z_{0}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]=e^{-2\alpha t}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{Z},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}.

(3) Convergence of mean-field min-max gradient descent:

Let
G\colon\mathbb{R}^{2d}\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
be
G(z)=z+\eta b^{Z}(z)
.
Note that
G(z^{*})=z^{*}
.
Recall from Lemma
30
that
G
is
M
-Lipschitz, where

M:=\sqrt{1-2\eta\alpha+4\eta^{2}L^{2}}\leq\sqrt{1-\eta\alpha}\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\eta\alpha}

where the bound above follows from the assumption
\eta\leq\frac{\alpha}{4L^{2}}
and the inequality
1-c\leq e^{-c}
.

From the update of mean-field min-max gradient descent (
72
), we can compute:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{x,\eta}}\left[\|x_{k+1}-x^{*}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{x,\eta}}\left[\left\|x_{k}-\eta\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{y,\eta}}[\nabla_{x}V(x_{k},y_{k})]-x^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}}\left[\left\|x_{k}-\eta\,\nabla_{x}V(x_{k},y_{k})-x^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

where the inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwarz and using
\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}=\rho_{k}^{x,\eta}\otimes\rho_{k}^{y,\eta}
.
Similarly, we can compute:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{y,\eta}}\left[\|y_{k+1}-y^{*}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{y,\eta}}\left[\left\|y_{k}+\eta\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{x,\eta}}[\nabla_{y}V(x_{k},y_{k})]-y^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}}\left[\left\|y_{k}+\eta\,\nabla_{y}V(x_{k},y_{k})-y^{*}\right\|^{2}\right].

Adding the two bounds above, and using
G(z)=z+\eta b^{Z}(z)
and
G(z^{*})=z^{*}
, we can bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}}\left[\|z_{k+1}-z^{*}\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}}\left[\left\|G(z_{k})-G(z^{*})\right\|^{2}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha\eta}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}}\left[\left\|z_{k}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]

where the last inequality follows from the property that
G
is
M
-Lipschitz with
M\leq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\eta}
.
Iterating the bound above gives:

\displaystyle W_{2}(\rho_{k}^{z,\eta},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{k}^{z,\eta}}\left[\left\|z_{k}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}^{z,\eta}}\left[\left\|z_{0}-z^{*}\right\|^{2}\right]=e^{-\alpha\eta k}\,W_{2}(\rho_{0}^{z,\eta},\delta_{z^{*}})^{2}

as desired.
∎
